# Milieutics
## History, Contingency, and the Realization of Possibility

### What Milieutics Is

**Milieutics** is the study of **how physically possible worlds become the worlds they actually are**.

Where other WMB domains describe:

- **what exists** (Ontics),    
- **what it is made of** (Morphotics),    
- **what kinds of animosys are possible** (Animotics),    
- **what physical bodies can host those possibilities** (Planemonics),    

**Milieutics asks the irreducible next question**:

> _Why did this world turn out this way — and not some other way it also could have?_

Milieutics concerns **realization**, not eligibility.  
It addresses **history**, not structure.  
It explains **outcomes**, not permissions.
### The Core Distinction: Possibility vs. Outcome

A central principle of WMB is that **structure does not determine history**.

- A world may be Biotic and remain sterile.    
- A world may be Terric and become hostile.    
- A world may be Geotic and never produce complex animosys.    
- A world may be nearly identical to Earth and diverge completely.    

All of these outcomes are **milieutic**, not animotic.

> **Animotics answers “could.”  
> Milieutics answers “did.”**

This separation is intentional.  
Without it, classification collapses into hindsight.
### What Milieutics Studies

Milieutics concerns **processes that unfold in time**, including:

- formation history    
- orbital evolution    
- stellar variability    
- volatile delivery and loss    
- obliquity and eccentricity changes    
- tectonic initiation and shutdown    
- atmospheric transitions    
- climate tipping points    
- catastrophic resets    
- path dependence and lock-in effects    

These are not properties of a world.  
They are **events** in a world’s story.

Milieutics therefore treats planets not as static objects, but as **historical trajectories through parameter space**.
### Directionality (Non-Negotiable)

Milieutics does **not** redefine other domains.

The arrow of implication is one-way:

> **Ontics → Morphotics → Animotics → Planemonics → Milieutics**

Milieutics:

- does **not** alter classifications,    
- does **not** redefine envelopes,    
- does **not** back-propagate outcomes into definitions.    

It explains how a world **moved within** the space defined by earlier constraints.

> **History operates inside structure — never in place of it.**
### Why Milieutics Is Necessary

Without Milieutics, worldbuilding (and planetary science) falls into one of two traps:

1. **Determinism**
    
    > “Given these parameters, this outcome was inevitable.”
    
2. **Narrative exceptionalism**
    
    > “This world is special because the story says so.”

Milieutics rejects both.

It insists instead that:

- multiple futures are always available,    
- most are never realized,    
- small differences compound,    
- timing matters,    
- reversibility is rare,    
- and outcomes are often fragile.    

> **A world’s present state is not its essence — it is its residue.**
### Milieutics and Fiction

Milieutics explicitly includes **fictional worlds**.

Not as speculation, but as **worked examples**.

When Milieutics examines:

- Earth through deep time,    
- the Solar System comparatively,    

it is not asking whether those worlds are “real.”

It is asking:

> _Given these stated constraints, do the outcomes follow — and if not, what would have to change?_

This makes Milieutics the natural home for:

- reconstructive analyses,    
- counterfactual histories,    
- diagnostic critiques,    
- and exemplar systems.    

These are **applications**, not deviations.
### Milieutics Is Not Prediction

Milieutics is retrospective, not prophetic.

It does not claim to forecast futures.  
It explains **why futures diverged**.

This distinction matters.

Prediction assumes control.  
Milieutics assumes humility.

> **The universe does not optimize.  
> It merely proceeds.**
### Ethics, Meaning, and Limits

Milieutics is **not moral philosophy**.

It does not declare outcomes good or bad.  
It does not assign purpose.  
It does not privilege comfort, intelligence, or survival.

It merely insists on one uncomfortable truth:

> **Awareness confers responsibility to account for consequence.**

Milieutics does not tell you what _should_ happen.  
It tells you what _did_ — and why you cannot pretend it happened otherwise.
### Summary (For Fast Readers)

- **Milieutics studies realization, not eligibility**    
- It explains how worlds move through possibility space over time    
- It is concerned with history, contingency, and irreversibility    
- It does not redefine other domains    
- It is the proper home for Earth histories, Solar System comparisons, and fictional analyses    
- It closes the WMB framework by addressing outcome without collapsing into hindsight    

Wimby does not claim the universe is fair, optimal, or kind.

Milieutics exists to show that **it is consistent — and that consistency has consequences**.

# Monon Morphotic Mapping

_When Morphology Becomes Structure_

**Monon Morphotic Mapping** describes **where and when morphotypes become structurally meaningful** across the mass–complexity continuum of monons.

It does **not** define morphotypes — those are specified in **[[Duromon Morphotics]]**.  
It does **not** classify sub-threshold composition — that is covered in **[[Micromon Compositional Regimes]]**.

Its purpose is to identify the **emergence thresholds** at which gravity enforces:

- differentiation    
- phase stability    
- internal layering    
- long-term structural memory    

In short:

> **This article answers the question:  
> “At what point does ‘what it’s made of’ become ‘what it is’?”**
## Conceptual Overview

Morphotics is not scale-invariant.

At small masses, composition exists but **structure does not persist**.  
At larger masses, gravity becomes the dominant organizing force, and **morphology stabilizes**.

Morphotic expression therefore:

- **emerges gradually**    
- **stabilizes discontinuously**    
- **depends on mass, not intent**    

This article maps that transition.
## Monon Superclasses and Morphotic Relevance

Monons fall into several superclasses based on binding regime and internal physics.  
Morphotics applies differently — or not at all — across these classes.

### **Monon Superclasses**

- **Synomons**  
    Cohesion without gravitation; morphotics does not apply.    
- **Duromons**  
    Gravity-dominated classical matter bodies; **formal morphotics emerges here**.    
- **Transomons**  
    Partially degenerate bodies; classical morphotypes give way to **ulsiform regimes**.    
- **Fusomons**  
    Fusion-powered bodies; morphotics is superseded by stellar physics.    
- **Peromons**  
    Relativistic or collapse-dominated structures; morphology is no longer planet-like.    

> **Formal morphotype expression stabilizes only within the duromonic regime.**
## Duromon Mass Regimes and Morphotic Emergence

Within the duromon superclass, morphotic behavior emerges progressively across mass subclasses.
### Micromons (≈10⁻⁶ – 10⁻⁵ t)

- Gravity insufficient for differentiation    
- Composition is descriptive, not structural    
- No stable morphotypes    
- Pre-morphotic regime    

_(See [[Micromon Compositional Regimes]])_
### Minimons (≈10⁻⁴ t)

- Weak gravitational ordering    
- Early, transient differentiation    
- Morphotic tendencies appear but do not persist    
### Midimons (≈10⁻³ t)

- First reliable internal differentiation    
- Stable lithic crusts may form    
- Rheiform behavior appears in volatile-rich bodies    
- Thin, transient aeriform envelopes possible    

> **Midimons mark the threshold where morphotics first becomes meaningful.**
### Mesamons (≈10⁻² t)

- Fully differentiated interiors    
- Stable lithiform structure    
- Persistent rheiform regimes in icy bodies    
- Early atmospheric retention
### Planemons (≈10⁻¹ – 10¹ t)

- Full morphotic maturity    
- Stable expression of:
       - Lithiform        
    - Rheiform        
    - Aeriform        
    - (contextually) Ulsiform components        
- Long-term tectonics, atmospheres, and internal energetics    

This is the domain of **Duromon Morphotics proper**.
## Morphotype Emergence by Regime

_(Threshold Summary)_

- **Lithiform**
    
    - First meaningful expression: **Midimons**        
    - Fully stable: **Mesamons and Planemons**
        
- **Rheiform**
    
    - Appears with internal melting or cryomelts        
    - Common in **icy Mesamons** and **outer-system Planemons**
        
- **Aeriform**
    
    - Requires sustained atmospheric retention        
    - First persistent expression: **Planemons**
        
- **Ulsiform**
    
    - Requires degenerate or exotic pressure regimes        
    - Appears only in **Transomons, Fusomons, and Peromons**
        

No morphotype appears “all at once.”  
Each requires sufficient gravity to **enforce order over time**.
## Key Principle: Emergence, Not Categorization

Morphotics is **emergent**, not intrinsic.

- Composition exists at all scales    
- Structure appears only when gravity dominates    
- Stability arrives only when time and mass align    

Thus:

- Micromons are **material archives**    
- Midimons are **structural experiments**    
- Planemons are **morphotic identities**    
## Cross-References

- **[[Duromon Morphotics]]** — formal morphotype definitions    
- **[[Micromon Compositional Regimes]]** — pre-morphotic composition    
- **[[Animotics Overview]]** — animotic classes layered atop lithiform bodies
## Summary

- Morphotics does not apply uniformly across scales    
- Stable morphotypes require duromonic mass regimes    
- This article maps **when morphology becomes structure**    
- It bridges Ontics and Morphotics without collapsing them    

> **Matter becomes form only when gravity insists.**

***

# The Mononic System  
## Structure, Cohesion, and Assemblies

**Mononics** is the study of self-coherent bodies — moons, planets, mesomons, stellamons, and degenerate remnants that populate the **Macrophysical Regime** of the WMB ontology.

Where the Ontic Regimes describe *how matter coheres* at different scales, Mononics begins the moment a body achieves **macrophysical identity**: the boundary, behavior, or effect that distinguishes it from its surroundings.

Monons are the building blocks of everything worldcrafters care about — and the units from which all larger systems are assembled.

## 1 · What Is a Monon?

A **Monon** is any *self-coherent* body with a distinguishable identity, whether fragile or massive, simple or complex.

A body is a monon if it:
- forms a **distinct boundary**,  
- behaves as a **unified whole**, or  
- exerts a **perceptible effect** distinguishable from its environment.

A snowflake, a droplet, a grain of sand, a boulder, a planemon, and a star all qualify — each at a different scale and via different binding mechanisms — but each achieves *unity*.

Monons divide into two fundamental structural types:

| **Type**    | **Binding**     | **Examples**                     |
| ----------- | --------------- | -------------------------------- |
| **Synomon** | Electrochemical | crystals, droplets, hailstones   |
| **Varymon** | Gravitational   | asteroids, moons, planets, stars |

**Synomons** are held together by molecular and crystalline forces.  
**Varymons** are held together by gravity overpowering electrochemical cohesion.

This distinction determines nearly everything about their behavior.

## 2 · Synomons — Electrochemically Bound Monons

Synomons inhabit the lower end of the Macrophysical Regime.  
Their unity is provided by **electrochemical cohesion**, not gravity.

Examples:
- a snowflake  
- a raindrop  
- a quartz crystal  
- a boulder  
- a gold nugget  
- a chunk of obsidian  

Synomons may be:
- **morphotically simple** (a pure diamond)  
- **morphotically structured** (granite, ice composites)

Synons combine into *synonic assemblies*, which are classified morphotically.

## 3 · Synonic Assemblies — Cules, Lexes, Gates

The suffixes **–cule**, **–lex**, and **–gate** describe how monons assemble into larger units. 
These are **morphotic distinctions**, describing sameness, variety, and context.

### 3.1 Synocule — Unity by Likeness

> A group of synomons sharing the **same morphotic nature**.

Examples:  
- a handful of diamonds  
- a pouch of quartz crystals  
- identically manufactured beads  

### 3.2 Synolex — Unity by Variation

> Synomons of *different morphotic expressions* of the same nature.

Example:  
Rubies + sapphires + emeralds + spinels → all minerals, different expressions.

### 3.3 Synogate — Unity by Context

> Synomons of **different morphotic natures** gathered as a contextual whole.

Examples:  
- a scoop of mixed gems + amber + obsidian + bone  
- beach sand (shells, minerals, silica fragments, organics)  
- a mixed rubble pile  

A synogate can be extremely complex — but still not self-gravitating.

## 4 · When Synogates Become Varymons — The Collapse Threshold

A synogate becomes a **varymon** when its mass is large enough that **self-gravity exceeds electrochemical cohesion**.

This generally occurs around:
-  **≈ 10⁻⁶ terran masses — the microterran threshold**

Below this:  
	**EC forces dominate → synonic structure.**

Above this:  
	**Gravity dominates → the body becomes a single varymonic monon**, regardless of heterogeneous composition.

This is how rubble piles become asteroids, icy conglomerates become moons, and protoplanets emerge from dust.
## 5 · Varymons — Gravibound Monons

A **Varymon** is any monon whose integrity comes from gravity.  
They dominate the astrophysical landscape:
- asteroids  
- dwarf planets  
- moons  
- terrestrial planets  
- gas/ice giants  
- mesomons (brown dwarfs)  
- stellamons (stars)  
- fusomons (fusion-powered stars)  
- peromons (collapsed objects)  

Varymons may be:

- **morphotically simple** (a carbon planet)  
- **morphotically complex** (layered worlds, differentiated bodies)

Gravitation becomes the master binder.

## 6 · Varymonic Assemblies — Varycule, Varylex, Varygate

As with synonic assemblies, the suffixes describe *structural organization*, not scale.
### 6.1 Varycule — Unity by Character

> Varymons of the **same morphotic character**.

Example:  
A cluster of S-type asteroids.

### 6.2 Varylex — Unity by Variety

> Varymons of *different morphotic varieties* within the same character.

Example:  
S-type + C-type asteroids in the same family.

### 6.3 Varygate — Unity by Context

> A gravitationally contextual collection of varymons of **different identities and natures**.

Examples:
- a planetary system  
- a binary star with planets  
- a debris field  
- an open cluster  
- a stellar nursery  
- a galaxy  
- a galaxy cluster  

**A varygate can be small or cosmic; the suffix defines structure, not size.**

## 7 · Summary Table — The Mononic Assembly Ladder

| **Level** | **Synonic Track** | **Varymonic Track** | **Binding Principle** |
| --------- | ----------------- | ------------------- | --------------------- |
| **Monon** | Synomon           | Varymon             | EC → Gravity          |
| **Cule**  | Synocule          | Varycule            | sameness              |
| **Lex**   | Synolex           | Varylex             | structured difference |
| **Gate**  | Synogate          | Varygate            | contextual coherence  |

A synogate transitions to a varymon when the gravitational threshold is crossed.  
Gates are scale-neutral — a puddle is a synogate; a galaxy is a varygate.

## 8 · Conceptual Arc

- **Synomons** → coherence through matter  
- **Varymons** → coherence through mass  
- **Cules** → unity by likeness  
- **Lexes** → unity by relationship  
- **Gates** → unity by context  

These structures form the backbone of Mononics, the physical ontology from which all crafted worlds emerge.

***

# What is a "moon"?

In WCB, the term **moon** is *synergological*; that is to say, it describes a **relationship** rather than a condition or status.

Accordingly, WCB defines *moon* as:

> “A natural mononic satellite of another monon that is one hundred or more times more massive than itself.”

The technical term for such a relationship is **parensate** — a monon gravitationally bound to another that overwhelmingly dominates the barycenter.

Thus, Titan is a *moon* of Saturn; and if Earth were in orbit around Jupiter, it too would be classed as a *moon*.

By contrast, if **Uranus** were in orbit around **Jupiter**, it would *not* be a moon ([[Principles and Properties|parensate]]) but a **sobrinate**, because the primaron mass ratio between the two is less than 100 : 1:

$$
\varphi = \frac{M_{Jupiter}}{M_{Uranus}} = \frac{317.8}{14.536} ≈ 21.9
$$

The singular term for a sobrinic pair is a **sobrinon**; for a parensic pair, a **parenson**.  We might therefore speak of the *Jupiter–Uranus sobrinon* or the *Saturn–Titan parenson*.

# Historical Context

## Etymology of "moon"

Most dictionaries are somewhat specific in defining the word “moon”: something along the lines of “a moon is a natural satellite of a planet”. However, modern exploration has shown this definition to be limiting: even asteroids have "moons" (Hebe 6 and $415$ others are known as of this writing). So, for our purposes, we need to be a bit more general.  WCB defines "moon" as  “a natural satellite of a monon more than 100 or more times more massive than itself.”

## "The" Moon

In English, the generic noun turned into a proper noun, “the Moon”, as a name for Earth’s only natural satellite. Other English names for the Moon, less commonly seen or used, include Luna, Selene, and Cynthia (the first is Latin, the others Greek). The most common adjectival form used to refer to the Moon is "lunar", though you might also sometimes see “selenic" and “cynthion" (with a hard ‘c’).

As a proper noun, the name “Moon” comes from Old English *mōna*, stemming from Proto-Germanic \**mēnōn*, itself from Proto-Indo-European (PIE) \**mēnsis*, which meant "month" (and still exists in English as “menses”), ultimately derived from PIE \**mēnōt* "to measure", because the Moon's phases were used to measure that unit of time.

In WCB, we will most often refer to Earth's Moon by the Roman name Luna, along with its related terms "lunar", "lunation", — 

For convenience, we'll often just use "moon" generically and more specific terms when distinction is called-for.

# Regular and Irregular

WCB retains the traditional astronomical distinction between **regular** and **irregular** moons (see below), while refining it through genealogical terms that clarify their origins: **primogenic** (formed alongside their primaron) and **syllipsic** (captured after formation).

**Regular** moons are described by the following characteristics:
- Orbit fairly close to the primaron
- Tend to have *prograde* orbits (they orbit in the same direction as the primaron's axial rotation)
	- With little-to-no inclination, and
	- Small eccentricities
- Are *primogenic*: they formed alongside their primaron

**Irregular** moons, in contrast, are described by the characteristics:
- Follow distant orbits
- Often have *retrograde* orbits (they orbit in the opposite direction from the primaron's rotation)
	- With high inclinations, and
	- High eccentricity
- Are *syllipsic*: they were captured by their primaron after forming elsewhere.

There are, of course, exceptions. Uranus’s moon **Triton** exhibits characteristics of both classes:  
- It orbits close to Uranus on a nearly circular path — traits of a *regular* moon.  
- Yet its orbit is **retrograde** and **highly inclined**, hallmarks of an *irregular* moon.

These contradictions imply that Triton is **syllipsic** in origin: a captured **midimon** (dwarf-planemon) whose orbit was later circularized by tidal evolution.

# Major and Minor

Traditionally, “major moons” are those that have achieved **hydrostatic equilibrium**—they are **spheric**, their shapes maintained primarily by gravity. “Minor moons,” in contrast, are **nodulic**: held together more by the tensile strength of their materials than by self-gravity.

These terms arose from **observational happenstance** rather than physical taxonomy. Larger, spherical moons were easier to detect with early telescopes and thus labeled “major.” The smaller, non-spherical, nodulose bodies were discovered later and labeled “minor.”

Historically, therefore, moons have been described within a **two-axis matrix**: *regular / irregular* vs. *major / minor.*

Examples include:  

- **Triton** — a *major–irregular* moon.  
- **Luna** — a *major–regular* moon.  
- **Phobos** and **Deimos** — *minor–irregular* moons.

The **Worldmaking Canon** deprecates *major* and *minor* as taxonomic labels. They imply **hierarchy** or **importance**, whereas in practice, they are only **observational artifacts**. A moon’s orbital configuration is the more meaningful attribute; its overall shape is an *emergent property* of its mass, density, and radius.

Accordingly, the Canon replaces “major” and “minor” with the intrinsic descriptors:  

- **spheric** — stable under self-gravity (hydrostatic equilibrium), and  
- **nodulic** — mechanically cohesive rather than gravitationally bound.

Thus:  

- **Triton** → *spheric, irregular parenson*  
- **Luna** → *spheric, regular parenson*  
- **Phobos** and **Deimos** → *nodulic, regular parensons*, being nonspherical yet orbiting close to Mars on low-inclination, nearly circular paths.
	
## Phobos and Deimos

Phobos and Deimos illustrate a point of divergence between the **Worldmaking Canon** and traditional astronomical nomenclature. Historically, both were classed as *minor–irregular* moons: small, nonspherical, and presumed to be **captured asteroids**.

Later analysis made that interpretation untenable.  Phobos, in particular, is too fragile to have survived an external capture; tidal stresses would have pulverized it before orbital stabilization.  
Current models indicate that both **Phobos** and **Deimos** are instead **retentive derivatives** of a vast **impact event** roughly **four billion years** ago, when a **Ceres–Vesta–mass** object collided with **Mars**.  The debris remained within Mars’s Hill sphere, briefly forming a transient ring system from which the present moons later accreted.

In Worldmaking Canon taxonomy, Phobos and Deimos are therefore **consequent, nodulic, regular parensons**—mechanically cohesive bodies in near-circular, low-inclination orbits that formed *as a result of* local events within their primaron’s domain.  They are *neither primogenic nor syllipsic*, but **consequent**, born of the Mars system’s own debris rather than imported from beyond it.

# Morphotics

**Morphotics** describes the **dominant matter regime** of a monon — what kind of material structure it expresses once coherence is achieved.

It applies to **all monons**, from synomons to peromons, but its expressions vary by mass regime.

- Field: **Morphotics**    
- Scope: **material composition, phase behavior, internal structure**    
- Independence: **orthogonal to animosys**    

> **Morphotics answers:**  
> _What is this body made of, structurally speaking?_

It does **not** answer:

- whether life exists    
- whether life is possible    
- whether a world is habitable    

Those are animotic overlays.
### Core Axes Summary

- **Mononic Axis** — _how_ a body coheres (electrochemical vs gravitational)    
- **Morphotic Axis** — _what kind of matter regime_ that coherence expresses    
- **Categorical Envelope** — how those traits behave within a system    

> Morphotics qualifies **substance**, not **outcome**.
## Morphotype

A **Morphotype** is the primary compositional and structural descriptor of a monon — its dominant material regime.

Canonical morphotypes include:

- **Lithiform** — solid rock–metal bodies    
- **Rheiform** — partially molten or cryo-fluid bodies    
- **Aeriform** — bodies dominated by gaseous or fluid envelopes    
- **Ulsiform** — degenerate or exotic matter regimes    

Morphotypes are **ontic**, not ecological.
## Monon Morphotic Mapping

This article maps **where morphotypes stabilize** across the mononic mass continuum.  
It does **not** define the morphotypes themselves.

Full morphotype definitions live in **[[Duromon Morphotics]]**.
### When Morphotics Becomes Meaningful

Morphotic expression requires:

- sufficient mass for differentiation    
- sustained internal energetics    
- stable phase domains    

This begins weakly in **midimons**, stabilizes in **mesamons**, and reaches full expression in **planemons**.
## Monon Superclasses (Context)

- **Synomons** — electrochemically bound; morphotics trivial or local    
- **Duromons** — gravitationally coherent classical matter bodies    
- **Transomons** — partially degenerate    
- **Fusomons** — fusion-dominated    
- **Peromons** — relativistic or collapse-dominated    

Classical morphotypes dominate **duromons**.  
**Ulsiform morphotics** dominates beyond them.
## Duromon Mass Subclasses

|Class|Approx. Mass|Morphotic Expression|
|---|---|---|
|Micromons|10⁻⁶–10⁻⁵ t|No stable differentiation|
|Minimons|~10⁻⁴ t|Weak, transient morphotics|
|Midimons|~10⁻³ t|First stable lithiformity|
|Mesamons|~10⁻² t|Full internal differentiation|
|Planemons|10⁻¹–10¹ t|Complete morphotic maturity|

Morphotics **matures with mass**, not time.
## Where Each Morphotype Appears

### Lithiform

- Requires mineral differentiation and pressure    
- First stabilizes in **midimons**    
- Fully expressed in **mesamons and planemons**    
### Rheiform

- Partial melt or cryo-fluid regimes    
- Common in volatile-rich mesamons and outer-system planemons    
- Often subsurface
### Aeriform

- Requires atmospheric retention    
- Appears at mesamon scale    
- Dominant in volatile-rich planemons    
### Ulsiform

- Requires degenerate or exotic pressure regimes    
- Appears only in **transomons, fusomons, and peromons**    
## Morphotypes vs Animotics

Morphotypes are **structural facts**.

- A lithiform world may be sterile    
- A rheiform world may host subsurface animosys    
- An aeriform world may support exotic ecosystems    
- An ulsiform body may host **Astatic animosys**    

> **Morphotics constrains animotics.  
> It never defines it.**
## Transition Rule (Critical)

A synonic assembly becomes a varymon when **self-gravity overwhelms electrochemical cohesion**.

At that moment:

- Morphotics transitions from local to global    
- Differentiation becomes inevitable    
- The body acquires a persistent material identity    

This is the ontic gateway to planetary structure.

## Comparison Modifiers
### Negation

- $\not{\;}$ overstruck on the operator — **negation**  

*Examples*:
```
	$\not=,\, \not\in,\, \not\approx,\, \not\le$  
```
yields: $\not=,\, \not\in,\, \not\approx,\, \not\le$
 
- **Plaintext alternative:** precede the operator with ! ⟶ !=, !∈, !≈, !≤

### Mandation

- $|$ before the operator — **mandative** (“must”)  

*Examples*:
```
	$|{\le}, \,  |{\in}, \, |{=}$  
```
yields: $|{\le}, \,  |{\in}, \, |{=}$  

### Prescription

- $|$ after the operator — **prescriptive** (“should”)  

*Examples*:
```
${\le}|,\, {\in}|, \, {=}|$  
```
yields: ${\le}|,\, {\in}|, \, {=}|$  

> *These symbols allow concise, unambiguous expression of conditional ranges and requirements in WCB equations.*

# 🧩 The Three Lexical Orders of WMB

WMB draws its vocabulary from three distinct “lexical orders,” each reflecting a different relationship between language and worldmaking intent. This taxonomy explains *how* terms enter the corpus and *why* they take the forms they do.
## 1. Neolex — New words the corpus invents for itself

**Definition:**  
A **Neolex** is a term coined outright within WMB — a true *neologism*.[^1]  
It expresses a concept that real-world language either lacks entirely or handles with unnecessary vagueness, complexity, or historical accident.

**Purpose:**  
To create precise, intuitive, reusable conceptual handles for worldmaking.

**Examples:**
- **Planemon, stellamon, duromon, peromon** — systematic mononic conformations.  
- **Tempostat** — the calendrical zero-point.  
- **Cascading Rounding Notation (CRN)** — a bespoke solution to a bespoke problem.  
- **GEWE** — a narrative-coherence principle that deserved a name.  
- **Resultant** — a general term for the post-operation value in calculations.  
- **Chronal Station, Sectal, Quartal** — time-geometry terminology invented for clarity.  
- **Morphotics, Conformics, Animotics, Milieutics** — named domains of worldmaking.

**Used when:**  
Clarity beats heritage, or no existing term expresses the idea cleanly.
## 2. Exolex — Borrowed intact from external science

**Definition:**  
An **Exolex** is a term imported without modification from astronomy, physics, mathematics, or another technical field. It arrives with its conventional meaning; WMB adapts to it, not the other way around.

**Purpose:**  
To avoid reinventing the wheel when a real-world term is already elegant and unambiguous.

**Examples:**
- **Roche limit**  
- **Hill sphere**  
- **Aphelion, periapsis** (used in their standard senses)  
- **Luminosity, albedo, eccentricity**  
- **Harmonics, resonances**

**Used when:**  
A real term is widely understood, precise, and fits WMB’s internal logic.

> [!note] The Indifference of Pragmatism  
> “Hill sphere” survives.  
> “Kozai–Lidov mechanism” silently wishes it could.

## 3. Insulex — Common words given specialized meanings inside WMB

**Definition:**  
An **Insulex** is an ordinary English word that WMB retools with a narrowed, technical sense appropriate to the corpus.

**Purpose:**  
To provide intuitive vocabulary that carries familiar connotations but has precise scope.

**Examples:**
- **Worldcrafting** — constructing the logosic content of reality.  
- **Worldbuilding** — shaping the mythosic context of living within it.  
- **Milieutics** — environmental and situational context of setting.  
- **Domain** — one of the six major structural areas of WMB.  
- **Station** — a specific position or moment in orbital geometry.

**Used when:**  
Familiarity helps comprehension, but precision is still required.

## ⭐ Why these three matter

Together, **Neolex**, **Exolex**, and **Insulex** create WMB’s vocabulary strategy:

- **Neolex** → invention for clarity  
- **Exolex** → inheritance for stability  
- **Insulex** → specialization for coherence  

This triad keeps WMB’s language expressive, intuitive, and structurally sound.
## The Structural Necessity of New Terms

As world systems grow, inherited vocabularies eventually reach their limits.  Real-world terminology was designed for real-world science, not for coherent fictional cosmologies, parametric conformations, or meta-mythic architectures. As WMB expanded, some concepts simply outgrew the language available to describe them.  

When the conceptual load exceeds the expressiveness of ordinary terminology, **new words become structurally necessary**, not ornamental.  Neolexicosis is thus not indulgence — it is the linguistic evolution required by a growing, internally consistent universe.

> [!tip] **A Worldmaker’s Immunity**
> Neolexicosis never really goes away.  
> The best a creator can do is manage the symptoms:
> - resist the urge when the old word truly works,  
> - embrace it when the new word brings clarity, and  
> - accept that sometimes your brain invents vocabulary faster than you can document it.
> 
> Worldmakers aren’t cured of Neolexicosis.  
> They simply learn to live *productively* with it.


[^1]: Greek: *néo-* “new” + *léxis* “word.”

🔝 [[01. What is WMB?]]

- Real moons often have **inclinations** relative to their planet’s equator.  
- Their declination, $\delta$, then varies not just with obliquity but also with orbital inclination ($i$) and the longitude of the ascending node $\Omega$:

$$
\delta \approx \arcsin\!\big(\sin i \cdot \sin(\lambda + \Omega)\big) \;+\; \epsilon \cos(2 \pi Z_f)
$$

- This can cause:  
  - Moons that **skim the horizon** at certain latitudes.  
  - Moons that **“vanish”** from view for part of their orbit.  
  - Irregular eclipse/transit opportunities.

# Flus Offset Factor (FOF)

The Flux Offset Factor (FOF) is a dimensionless measure of stellar irradiance at a given orbit relative to the nucleal orbit, expressed as a signed offset. The nucleal orbit is defined as the reference orbit (FOF = 0), corresponding to the stellar irradiance received by Earth around the Sun.

Positive values of FOF indicate _intranucleal_ excess stellar flux, while negative values indicate _extranucleal_ flux deficit. FOF does not itself describe habitability; rather, it provides a physically grounded coordinate from which habitability-related interpretations may be derived.

From a human-centered perspective, an Earth-like planet at or near the nucleal orbit has the highest likelihood of Earth-like surface conditions, all else being equal.

$$
\begin{align}
FOF &= \frac{N}{D^2} - 1 \\[1ex]
\text{But, since N} &:= \text{1 in this instance, the equation simplifies to:} \\[1ex]
FOF &= \frac{1}{D^2} - 1 
\end{align}
$$

Where:
- $FOF$ = the numeric value of the Flux Offset Factor
- *$D$* = the orbit's distance in AU (relative to the nucleal orbit)

Values of $FOF > 0$ indicate that the orbit is _intranucleal_ and values of $FOF < 0$ indicate that the orbit is extranucleal.  The total range of $FOF \in (-1, \infty)$, but for our purposes the limiting range is $FOF \in (-0.96, 3.0)$, as tabulated below

| Orbit<br>Type | Orbit<br>Distance |  FOF  |
| :-----------: | :---------------: | :---: |
| Intranucleal  |     0.500$N$      | 3.00  |
| Intranucleal  |     0.750$N$      | 0.78  |
| Intranucleal  |     0.950$N$      | 0.10  |
|    Nucleal    |     1.000$N$      | 0.00  |
| Extranucleal  |     1.385$N$      | -0.48 |
| Extranucleal  |     1.770$N$      | -0.68 |
| Extranucleal  |     4.850$N$      | -0.96 |
Values of $FOF>0$ indicate that an orbit is _intranucleal_, while values of $FOF<0$ indicate that it is _extranucleal_. In principle, the Flux Offset Factor spans the range $FOF \in (−1,\infty)$, approaching $−1$ asymptotically with increasing orbital distance and diverging without bound as distance decreases.

For practical purposes within Wimby, analysis is typically restricted to the interval $FOF \in (−0.96, 3.0)$, corresponding to the conventional outer and inner orbital bounds tabulated below.

![[_assets/images/Flux Offset Factor Graph.png]]

**Flux Offset Factor (FOF) as a function of normalized orbital distance.**  
FOF expresses stellar irradiance at a given orbit relative to the nucleal orbit ($FOF=0$). Positive values indicate intranucleal excess flux, while negative values indicate extranucleal flux deficit. The curve asymptotically approaches $−1$ with increasing orbital distance and diverges without bound as orbital distance decreases.

## Normalized Equilibrium Temperature

While the Flux Offset Factor (FOF) describes how stellar irradiance varies with orbital distance, **temperature responds to irradiance nonlinearly**.  As a result, temperature varies much more gradually than flux across orbital space.

$$
\begin{align}
NET &= \frac{1}{\sqrt{D}} \\[1ex]

\end{align}
$$

Where:
- $NET$ = the numeric value of the orbit's Normalized Equilibrium Temperature
- *$D$* = the orbit's distance in AU (relative to the nucleal orbit)

![[FOF Temperature Graph.png]]

**Normalized equilibrium temperature as a function of normalized orbital distance.**  
The curve shows equilibrium temperature $T/T_N$​ derived from stellar irradiance via Stefan–Boltzmann scaling, assuming an airless body in radiative equilibrium. Temperature changes more gradually than flux, rising rapidly at small orbital distances and declining slowly with increasing distance. The curve passes through the nucleal orbit at $(\dfrac{D}{N} = 1,\; \dfrac{T}{T_N} = 1)$ and approaches zero asymptotically at large distances.

FOF:Two features of this curve are especially important:

1. **Thermal buffering relative to flux.**  
    Although stellar flux increases or decreases rapidly with orbital distance, temperature responds more slowly. Large changes in irradiance correspond to comparatively modest changes in equilibrium temperature. This buffering effect explains why atmospheres, albedo, and heat transport can substantially modify surface conditions without altering the underlying stellar energy budget.
    
2. **Asymmetric thermal response.**  
    As with flux, the temperature curve is asymmetric about the nucleal orbit. Inward motion produces relatively rapid heating, while outward motion results in a long, shallow decline. Even at large distances, equilibrium temperature approaches zero only asymptotically, never reaching it.
    
| Orbit<br>Type | Orbit<br>Distance |  NET   |
| :-----------: | :---------------: | :----: |
| Intranucleal  |     0.500$N$      | 1.4142 |
| Intranucleal  |     0.750$N$      | 1.1547 |
| Intranucleal  |     0.950$N$      | 1.0260 |
|    Nucleal    |     1.000$N$      | 1.0000 |
| Extranucleal  |     1.385$N$      | 0.8497 |
| Extranucleal  |     1.770$N$      | 0.7516 |
| Extranucleal  |     4.850$N$      | 0.4541 |

Like FOF, NET is **purely physical**. It does not encode assumptions about atmospheres, phase states of water, biology, or habitability. Instead, it provides a second, derived coordinate that translates stellar energy availability into thermal terms. Thermozones, animozones, and anthropic interpretations are applied subsequently as overlays, not embedded in the temperature function itself.

Together, FOF and NET form a complementary pair: FOF describes how much energy is available, while $T/T_N$​ shows how that energy expresses itself thermally.

# Number Range Notation

WMB employs a compressed symbolic notation for expressing range relationships, logical connections, and modal constraints. This keeps all operations in a consistent visual and syntactic format. The sections below define these conventions in both rendered and plaintext forms (when needed).

## Range Connectives

**Interior (Conjunction)**:

* *Inclusive-Inclusive*
$$(a \leq x;\;x  \leq b)$$
- Value must lie **inside** the range between *a* and *b*, **including both** *a* and *b*.

* *Exclusive-Inclusive*
$$(a < x;\;x \leq b)$$
* Value must lie **inside** the range between *a* and *b*, **excluding** *a* and **including** *b*.

* *Inclusive-Exclusive*
$$(a \leq x;\;x < b)$$
* Value must lie **inside** the range between *a* and *b*, **including** *a* and **excluding** *b*.

* *Exclusive-Exclusive*
$$(a < x;\;x < b)$$
* Value must lie **inside** the range between *a* and *b*, **excluding both** *a* and *b*.

**Exterior (Disjunction)**:

- *Inclusive-Inclusive*
$$(x \leq a;\;x \geq b)$$
* Value must lie **outside** the range between *a* and *b*, **including both** *a* and *b*.

* *Exclusive-Inclusive*
$$(x < a;\;x \geq b)$$
* Value must lie **outside** the range between *a* and *b*, **excluding** *a* and **including** *b*.

* *Inclusive-Exclusive*
$$(x \leq a;\;x > b)$$
* Value must lie **outside** the range between *a* and *b*, **including** *a* and **excluding** *b*.

* *Exclusive-Exclusive*
$$(x < a;\;x > b)$$
*  Value must lie **outside** the range between *a* and *b*, **excluding both** *a* and *b*.

# Obliquity — Planetary Orientation

## Orientation of the Orbital Normal

**Orbital Normal ($\mathbb{N}$)**
The vector perpendicular to the orbital plane, defined by the **right-hand rule**: when the fingers of the right hand curl in the direction of **orbital motion**, the extended thumb points toward **orbital north**. Formally:

$$
\mathbb{N} = \frac{\mathbf{r} \,\boldsymbol{\times}\, \mathbf{v}}{|\mathbf{r} \,\boldsymbol{\times}\, \mathbf{v}|}
$$

This establishes a concrete mathematical object, such that:

$$
\begin{array}{l c l}
	+ x &= &\text{barycenter} \rightarrow \text{periapsis} \\[0.25em]
 + y &= &\text{direction of motion \textit{at} periapsis} \\[0.25em]
	+ z &= &\mathbf{N}
\end{array}
$$

### Why the Right-hand Rule is Necessary

In an isotropic universe there’s no intrinsic “up,” “north,” or “clockwise.” Every plane in space has *two* equally valid perpendiculars, and they point in opposite directions. Without an external asymmetry (a magnetic field, angular momentum, etc.), you can’t say which one is “positive.”

The right-hand rule supplies that missing **orientation convention**:
* If you curl the fingers of your right hand in the direction of orbital motion,
* Your thumb points along the **positive normal** — the **orbital north**.

This convention doesn’t imply that the cosmos *prefers* that direction; it merely provides a shared geometric rule so we can speak consistently about “north,” “tilt,” and “anticlockwise.”

## Defining Obliquity

Once $\mathbf{N}$ is defined, **obliquity** ($\varepsilon$) becomes an immediate vector relationship:

$$
\begin{gathered}
\\cos \varepsilon = \mathbf{s} \!\cdot\! \mathbf{N}, \\[1em]
\mathbf{s} =
\bigl(
\sin|\varepsilon| \cos\phi,\,
\sin|\varepsilon| \sin\phi,\,
\operatorname{sgn}(\varepsilon)\cos|\varepsilon|
\bigr)
\end{gathered}
$$

Where:
- $\phi$ = the **solstitial angle**, the angular offset of the spin-axis projection from the +x-axis (the barycenter–periapsis line) within the orbital plane.

This ties tilt, azimuth, and the coordinate frame together cleanly.

## Obliquity Progression

Since obliquity is the **instantaneous angle** between a monon’s rotational axis and the perpendicular to its orbital plane, it is necessary to indicate how the current state of the obliquity is varying. An up arrow $\uparrow$ or down arrow $\downarrow$ may be appended after the angular measure to indicate whether the obliquity is increasing or decreasing. 

For Earth: 

$$
\varepsilon = 23.5^\circ\downarrow
$$
## Obliquity Envelope ($\varepsilon_\eta$) 

$$
\varepsilon_\eta =
\begin{bmatrix}
\varepsilon_{min} \\
\varepsilon_{mean} \\
\varepsilon_{max}
\end{bmatrix}
$$

Where: 
- $\varepsilon_x$ = current tilt angle 
- $\varepsilon_{min}$ = minimum obliquity 
- $\varepsilon_{mean}$ = mean obliquity 
- $\varepsilon_{max}$ = maximum obliquity 

Example (Earth): 

$$
\varepsilon_\eta = 
\begin{bmatrix}
22.1^\circ \\
23.3^\circ \\
24.5^\circ
\end{bmatrix}
$$

## Obliquity Span ($\varepsilon_\delta$)

Simply the difference between the maximum and minimum obliquity extents:

$$
\varepsilon_\delta = \varepsilon_{max} - \varepsilon_{min}
$$

For Earth:

$$
\varepsilon_\delta = 24.5^\circ - 22.1^\circ = 2.4^\circ
$$

## Obliquity Cycle ($\varepsilon_\sigma$) 

The time interval between two maxima (or minima) of obliquity. 

For Earth: 

$$
\varepsilon_\sigma \approx 41{,}000 \text{ years}
$$

## Obliquity Tempo ($\varepsilon_\tau$) 

Rate of obliquity change per year (chronum): 

$$
\varepsilon_\tau = \dfrac{\varepsilon_{max} - \varepsilon_{min}}{\varepsilon_\sigma}
$$

For Earth: 

$$
\varepsilon_\tau = \frac{24.5 - 22.1}{41000} ≈ 0.0000585^\circ/\text{yr} \;=\; 0.00585^\circ/\text{kyr}
$$
## Obliquity Magnitude ($\varepsilon_\rho$) 

The ratio of the current obliquity to its maximum value, expressed as a percentage, with an arrow showing whether the trend is increasing $\uparrow$ or decreasing $\downarrow$:

$$
\varepsilon_\rho = \dfrac{\varepsilon}{\varepsilon_{max}}\times 100 \; (\uparrow,\downarrow)
$$

For Earth: 

$$
\varepsilon_\rho = \dfrac{23.5}{24.5}\times 100 \approx 95.9\text{\%}\;\downarrow
$$

# Orbital Terms (updated)

**Gyrostat** ($\Gamma_1$) — name and true anomaly of the Prime Solstice (see below); can be calculated from the chronostat by: $\Gamma = \Lambda - 90$.
**Chronostat** ($\Lambda_1$) — name and true anomaly of the Prime Equinox; determines the start of the planemon's seasonal year.
**Gyropode** ($\Gamma_2$) — name and true anomly of the Secondary Solstice.
**Chronopode** ($\Lambda_2$) — name and true anomaly of the Secondary Equinox
**Orbinodes** — the collective name of the gyrostat, chronostat, gyropode, and chronopode, the true anomalies of the sectal onsets.
**Peristat** ($\Phi$) — the _reference position_ where the Prime Solstice (gyrostat) is aligned with *periapsis*; marks the beginning of the Precession Cycle (Ψ)
**Apostat** ($\Phi_2$) — when Prime Solstice (gyrostat) is aligned with _apoapsis_; not regularly used, but included for completeness.
**Precession Cycle** (Ψ) — the interval between successive _peristats_; in physical terms: the time required for the spin axis to precess so that the **gyrostat again aligns with periapsis**.
**Precession Phase** (ψ) — the current percentage of the Precession Cycle (where the true anomaly of the gyrostat falls in the planmon's orbit); calculated by $$\psi = \frac{(\Lambda_1 - 90) \mod 360}{360} \cdot \Psi$$**Sectastat** (ζ) — name and true anomaly of the **first seasonal event after periapsis**; this angle is the origin of the **orbital secta**, which divide the orbit according to orbital velocity. The sectustat is always one of the four orbinodes.
**Chronum** (χ) — orbital period of the planemon (expressed in Earth sidereal years)
**Eccentricity** (e) — expresses variation from circularity of the planemon's orbit
**True Anomaly** (ν) — the *spatial angle* between periapsis and the planet’s actual position, measured from the system’s focus. Some true anomalies are associated with specific locations on the orbit where significant geometric transisions occur, but every position of a planet along its orbital path corresponds to a specific true-anomaly angle.
**Eccentric Anomaly** (E) — the *geometric angle* measured at the ellipse’s center to the projected point on its circumscribed circle
**Mean Anomaly** (ς) — the *temporal angle* that increases uniformly with time, defining a constant angular rate around the orbit.
**Eccentric Tangent Factor** (ξ) – a dimensionless helper constant linking *true* and *eccentric* anomalies through their tangent relation, calculated by $$\xi = \sqrt{\frac{1 - e}{1 + e}}$$
— links true and eccentric anomalies
**Obliquity** (ε) — _axial tilt_ between the spin vector and orbital normal.
**Prime Solstice** — the position in a planemon's orbit when its rotational axis is tilted directly away from the barymetric center of the system, per the right-hand rule; gyrostat
**Prime Equinox** — the position in a planemon's orbit when the right ascension of the barymetric center of the system crosses the _ascending node_; chronostat
**Obliquity Progression** — an arrow ↑ or ↓ appended to a planemon's obliquity angle indicating whether obliquity is increasing or decreasing.
**Obliquity Envelope** ($\varepsilon_\eta$) — notation of the minimum, mean, and maximum obliquity angles, denoted by

$$
\begin{aligned}
\varepsilon_\eta &:=
\begin{bmatrix}
  \varepsilon_{min} \\
  \varepsilon_{mean} \\
  \varepsilon_{max}
\end{bmatrix}, \text{ or} \\[2ex]
\varepsilon_\eta &:= [\varepsilon_{min}, \varepsilon_{mean}, \varepsilon_{max}]
\end{aligned}
$$

**Obliquity Span** ($\varepsilon_\delta$) — the difference between $\varepsilon_{max} \text{ and } \varepsilon_{min}$
**Obliquity Cycle** ($\varepsilon_\sigma$) — the interval between two occurrences of the same obliquity angle ($\varepsilon$)
**Obliquity Tempo** ($\varepsilon_\tau$) — the rate of change of the obliquity cycle

$$
\varepsilon_\tau = \dfrac{\varepsilon_{max} - \varepsilon_{min}}{\varepsilon_\sigma}
$$

**Obliquity Magnitude** ($\varepsilon_\rho$) — The ratio of the current obliquity to its maximum value, expressed as a percentage, with an arrow showing whether the trend is increasing $\uparrow$ or decreasing $\downarrow$:

$$
\varepsilon_\rho = \dfrac{\varepsilon}{\varepsilon_{max}}\times 100 \; (\uparrow,\downarrow)
$$

**Secta** ($S_0, S_1, S_2, S_3$) — the four unequal segments of the orbit partitioned by orbital velocity (i.e., by Kepler’s Second Law).  _Secta_ are _not seasons_.  Seasons occur within secta... they may coincide if the sectustat = the chronostat ($\zeta = \Lambda_1$) , but will offset otherwise.
- When you calculate secta, you calculate season lengths, you just have to determine which sectal corresponds to which season by noting the relationship of the chronostat to the sectastat. If you want to make it easy on yourself, set ζ = Λ and Spring is S₀, etc.

***
Tag Core
#ontics #orbits #time #math #planemonics

# Fleshing Out A Star System

We've established:
- [[Stars and Spectral Classes|Stars and Spectral Classes]]
- [[Fundamental Orbits, Thermozones, and Animozones|Fundamental Orbits, Thermozones, and Animozones]]
- [[(DEPR) 12. Orbital Habitability Index|Orbital Habitability Index]]
- [[Fundamental Orbits, Thermozones, and Animozones|Nucleal]] ($N$) Orbit, and
- [[Fundamental Orbits, Thermozones, and Animozones|Perannual]] ($P$) Orbit

But planemons don't orbit only at these discreet distances – they're all over the place. Here's a breakdown of our own Solar system's planemon orbit data:

$$
\begin{array}{l | r | r}
\textbf{Planemon} & \textbf{$SMA^*$ (AU)} & \textbf{$e^†$}\hspace{1ex} \\
\hline
\textbf{Mercury} & 0.387 & 0.2056 \\[1ex]
\textbf{Venus} & 0.723 & 0.0068 \\[1ex]
\textbf{Earth} & 1 & 0.0167 \\[1ex]
\textbf{Mars} & 1.524 & 0.0934 \\[1ex]
\textbf{Asteroids} & (2.2 \cdot\cdot\; 3.2) & μ = 2.7 \\[1ex]
\textbf{Jupiter} & 5.204 & 0.0489 \\[1ex]
\textbf{Saturn} & 9.583 & 0.0565 \\[1ex]
\textbf{Uranus} & 19.191 & 0.2056 \\[1ex]
\textbf{Neptune} & 30.070 & 0.0087 \\[1ex]
\end{array}
$$
> >$\small *\; SMA = \text{semi-major axis}$
> >$\small †\; e = \text{orbital eccentricity}$
## Orbital Parameters

Ignoring the bulk of the asteroid belt for the moment and inserting Ceres as the largest member of the belt:

$$
\begin{array}{l | r | r | r | r}
\textbf{Planemon} & \textbf{$SMA^*$ (AU)} & \textbf{$e^†$}\hspace{1ex} & \textbf{Gap (AU)} & \textbf{Interval (AU)} \\
\hline
\textbf{Mercury} & 0.387 & 0.2056 & —  &  — \\[1ex]
\textbf{Venus} & 0.723 & 0.0068 & 0.3362 & 1.8686 \\[1ex]
\textbf{Earth} & 1 & 0.0167 & 0.2767 & 1.3825 \\[1ex]
\textbf{Mars} & 1.524 & 0.0934 & 0.5237 & 1.5237 \\[1ex]
\textbf{Ceres} & 2.7 & 0.15 & 0.1763 & 1.7720 \\[1ex]
\textbf{Jupiter} & 5.204 & 0.0489 & 2.5038 & 1.9273 \\[1ex]
\textbf{Saturn} & 9.583 & 0.0565 & 4.3788 & 1.8415 \\[1ex]
\textbf{Uranus} & 19.191 & 0.2056 & 9.6087 & 2.0227 \\[1ex]
\textbf{Neptune} & 30.070 & 0.0087 & 10.8787 & 1.5669 \\[1ex]
\end{array}
$$

>Notes:
>\* The orbital **gap** is calculated by subtracting the planemon's orbital distance from the next closer-in planemon's orbital distance
> 
> $$G = O_n - O_{n-1}$$
> >† The orbital **interval** is calculated by dividing the planemon's orbital distance by the next closer-in planemon's orbital distance:
> 
> $$I = \dfrac{O_n}{O_{n-1}}$$

What we are concerned with here is the orbital *intervals*:
- The minimum interval is between Venus and Earth: $I = 1.3825\;AU$
- The maximum interval is between Uranus and Neptune: $I = 2.0027\;AU$
- The average interval is $I \approx 1.74\;AU$
- The median interval is $I \approx 1.81\;AU$
- The standard deviation is $\sigma = 0.2051$

This supports defining a **WMB-conservative orbital interval range** of $(1.400 ·· 2.000)$ AU by rounding up the minimum and rounding down the maximum.

However, a cursory survey of the [Exoplanet Catalog](https://www.google.com/url?sa=t&rct=j&q=&esrc=s&source=web&cd=&ved=2ahUKEwja9NLJi7uRAxWtBbwBHR_tEYgQFnoECB0QAQ&url=https%3A%2F%2Fscience.nasa.gov%2Fexoplanets%2Fexoplanet-catalog%2F&usg=AOvVaw1RgK4I4ZHZhrvHlQcvBx9J&opi=89978449) seems to reveal a range of $(1.000 ··\; 5.000)$ AU for planetary orbital intervals.  We'll define this as our optimistic orbital interval range, and, for completeness, average the two ranges at $(1.200 ··\; 3.500)$ AU.

**Orbital interval ranges**

| Inner | Outer | Description  |
| :---: | :---: | ------------ |
|  1.4  |   2   | Conservative |
|  1.2  |  3.5  | Medial       |
|   1   |   5   | Optimistic   |
# Calculating Other Orbits

This brings us to methods of calculating other orbits in a star system.

In practice, any method the worldmaker chooses is *valid*, including just putting planemons where *it feels right*; however, even using this "pickup sticks" method *should* ideally take into account the above statistics and try to avoid an orbital interval between a given planemon and its nearest neighbor of $< 1.500\;AU$ or $> 2.000\;AU$.

## Starting From A Known Orbit

Most of the time, you'll have pre-established a particular orbit — usually either the *nucleal* or the *perannual* orbit, and want to arrange other planemons in the system relative to that orbit. Notating this orbit as the **base** orbit, we can set up two processes for calculating *intrabasal* orbits (inferior to; closer-in than) and *extrabasal* orbits (superior to; farther-out than) that orbit.

**Intrabasal Orbit Calculation Process**

$$
r_i = B;\; \Omega = \text{«▢»}: \qquad
r_{i-1} = \frac{r_i}{\langle \text{min} \leq x; x \leq \text{max} \rangle}
\quad \text{while } r_{i-1} ≥ \Omega
$$
**Extrabasal Orbit Calculation Process**

$$
r_i = B;\; \Omega = \text{«▢»}: \qquad
r_{i+1} = r_i \cdot \langle \text{min} \leq x; x \leq \text{max} \rangle
\quad \text{while } r_{i+1} ≤ \Omega
$$
Where:
- *B* = basal orbital radius (e.g. the nucleal orbit $N$)
- *Ω* = orbital distance cutoff (minimum or maximum allowed orbit based on the star system constraints)

## Usage Strategy

*Assuming the **medial** orbital interval range* $I \in ((1.200 ··\; 3.500))\;AU$:
The **intrabasal** and **extrabasal** forms can be used independently depending on your desired anchoring strategy:

> **Inward-Only Generation** 
 If you begin at the **basal orbit** (innermost, nucleal, perannual, etc.), use the **intrabasal** form to expand inward via divisive steps:

$$
 r_0 = B;\; \Omega = «▢»:
 \quad r_{i-1} = \dfrac{r_i} {\langle 1.200 \leq x; x \leq 3.500 \rangle}, \text{ while } r_{i-1} ≥ \Omega
$$
Where:
- *Ω* = the minimum safe orbital distance — usually taken to be $a = 0.100\;AU$.

> **Outward-Only Generation** 
 If you begin at an **innermost orbit** (e.g. a thermal, Roche, or design constraint), use the **intrabasal** form to expand outward via multiplicative steps:

$$
 r_0 = B;\; \Omega = «▢»:
 \quad r_{i+1} = r_i \cdot \langle 1.200 \leq x; x \leq 3.500 \rangle, \text{ while } r_{i+1} ≤ \Omega
$$
Where:
- *Ω* = the farthest orbit desired for a planemon in the system — based on whatever criterion desired, but physically limited to the Hill Sphere radius of the central mass.

> [!tip]
> > Applying these methods can fully define a system.
> > - If one wanted to start with the innermost safe orbit; e.g. $B = 0.100\;AU$, then one would use only the extrabasal process to calculate orbits outward.
> > - Conversely if one wanted to start with the most distant orbit, then one would use only the intrabasal process to calculate orbits inward.


> [!important]
> >> *If you are not starting from the perannual or nucleal orbit, always check randomized orbits against either (or both) to ensure proper interval **gaps** fall to either side of that orbit, and adjust accordingly!*

## A Worked Example

Let us say we've identified our nucleal orbit ($N$) as $N = 0.834\;AU$, and we want to calculate orbits interior-to and exterior-to that orbit, and we've chosen $a = 0.100\;AU$ as our innermost safe orbit.

### Working Inward
$$
\begin{aligned}
 r_0 &= 0.834;\; \Omega = 0.100:
 \quad r_{i-1} = \dfrac{r_i}{\langle1.200 \le x; x \le 3.500\rangle}, \text{ while } r_{i-1} ≥ 0.100 \\
r_{i-1} &= \dfrac{0.834} {1.723} = 0.482\;AU \\[1ex]
&\quad 1.732 := \text{Randomized interval between $\langle1.200 \le x; x \le 3.500\rangle$ AU} \\[1ex]
r_{i-1} &= \dfrac{0.482} {1.616} = 0.298\;AU \\[1ex]
&\quad 1.616 := \text{Randomized interval between $\langle1.200 \le x; x \le 3.500\rangle$ AU} \\[1ex]
r_{i-1} &= \dfrac{0.298} {1.573} = 0.190\;AU \\[1ex]
&\quad 1.573 := \text{Randomized interval between $\langle1.200 \le x; x \le 3.500\rangle$ AU} \\[1ex]
r_{i-1} &= \dfrac{0.190} {1.884} = 0.101\;AU \\[1ex]
&\quad 1.884 := \text{Randomized interval between $\langle1.200 \le x; x \le 3.500\rangle$ AU} \\[1ex]
r_{i-1} &= \dfrac{0.101} {1.963} = 0.051\;AU \\[1ex]
&\quad 1.963 := \text{Randomized interval between $\langle1.200 \le x; x \le 3.500\rangle$ AU}\;✘ \\[1ex]
\end{aligned}
$$

We stop at the fourth randomized orbit, because the next orbit randomly generated fails the $r ≥ 0.100\;AU$ test.
We now have a system of five orbits:

| Orbit | Distance |
| :-----: | :------: |
| 1 | 0.101 |
| 2 | 0.190 |
| 3 | 0.298 |
| 4 | 0.482 |
| 5 (*N*) | 0.834 |

We could stop here and have a fully legitimate star system, but let's say we want extranucleal orbits, as well. Again, beginning with the nucleal orbit $B = 0.834\;AU$, and setting an outermost orbit of $\Omega = 35.0\;AU$:

$$
\begin{aligned}
 r_0 &= 0.834;\; \Omega = 35.0 \\[1ex]
 \quad r_{i+1} &= r_i \cdot \langle1.200 \le x; x \le 3.500\rangle, \text{ while } r_{i+1} ≤ 35.0 \\[1ex]
r_{i+1} &= 0.834(1.829) = 1.525\;AU \\ 
&\quad 1.829 := \text{Randomized interval between $\langle1.200 \le x; x \le 3.500\rangle$ AU} \\[1ex]
r_{i+1} &= 1.525(1.969) = 3.003\;AU \\
&\quad 1.969 := \text{Randomized interval between $\langle1.200 \le x; x \le 3.500\rangle$ AU} \\[1ex]
r_{i+1} &= 3.003(1.578) = 4.739\;AU \\
&\quad 1.578 := \text{Randomized interval between $\langle1.200 \le x; x \le 3.500\rangle$ AU} \\[1ex]
r_{i+1} &= 4.739(1.547) = 7.332\;AU \\
&\quad 1.547 := \text{Randomized interval between $\langle1.200 \le x; x \le 3.500\rangle$ AU} \\[1ex]
r_{i+1} &= 7.332(1.552) = 11.379\;AU \\
&\quad 1.552 := \text{Randomized interval between $\langle1.200 \le x; x \le 3.500\rangle$ AU} \\[1ex]
r_{i+1} &= 11.379(1.608) = 18.298\;AU \\
&\quad 1.608 := \text{Randomized interval between $\langle1.200 \le x; x \le 3.500\rangle$ AU} \\[1ex]
r_{i+1} &= 18.298(1.823) = 33.357\;AU \\
&\quad 1.823 := \text{Randomized interval between $\langle1.200 \le x; x \le 3.500\rangle$ AU} \\[1ex]
r_{i+1} &= 33.357(1.778) = 59.309\;AU \\
&\quad 1.778 := \text{Randomized interval between $\langle1.200 \le x; x \le 3.500\rangle$ AU} \; ✘ \\[1ex]
\end{aligned}
$$
We stop at the seventh iteration, as the next value exceeds $\Omega = 35.0\;AU$.

This expands our system to 11 orbits:

| Orbit | Distance |
| :-----: | :------: |
| 1 | 0.101 |
| 2 | 0.190 |
| 3 | 0.298 |
| 4 | 0.482 |
| 5 (*N*) | 0.834 |
| 6 | 1.525 |
| 7 | 3.003 |
| 8 | 4.739 |
| 9 | 11.379 |
| 10 | 18.298 |
| 11 | 33.357 |

… and we can proceed to design planemons for each orbit, or eliminate some orbits and install asteroid belts, or adjust orbital intervals to suit our needs.... the sky's the limit.

With this method, a worldmaker can quickly generate a full planemon system that is physically plausible, statistically grounded, and symbolically consistent with WMB cosmology.

# Calculating the Thermozones
Since we know our nucleal orbit is $N = 0.834\;AU$, we can calculate the thermozone limits:

$$
\begin{aligned}
H_0 = 0.500N &= 0.500(0.834) = 0.417\;AU \\[1ex]
H_1 = 0.750N &= 0.750(0.834) = 0.626\;AU \\[1ex]
H_2 = 0.950N &= 0.950(0.834) = 0.792\;AU \\[1ex]
H_3 = 1.385N &= 1.385(0.834) = 1.115\;AU \\[1ex]
H_4 = 1.770N &= 1.770(0.834) = 1.476\;AU \\[1ex]
H_5 = 4.850N &= 4.850(0.834) = 4.045\;AU \quad \text{Frost Line}
\end{aligned}
$$

And we can add these to our orbits table from above and determine the thermozones and animozones of the orbits:

|  Orbit   | Distance | <center>Thermozone</center> | <center>Animozone</center> |
| :------: | :------: | --------------------------- | -------------------------- |
|    1     |  0.101   | Igniozone                   | Xenotic                    |
|    2     |  0.190   | Igniozone                   | Xenotic                    |
|    3     |  0.298   | Ignoizone                   | Xenotic                    |
|  $H_0$   |  0.417   |                             |                            |
|    4     |  0.482   | Calorozone                  | Inner Parahabitable        |
|  $H_1$   |  0.626   |                             |                            |
|  $H_2$   |  0.792   |                             |                            |
| 5 (*$N$) |  0.834   | Solarazone                  | Hospitable                 |
|  $H_3$   |  1.115   |                             |                            |
|  $H_4$   |  1.476   |                             |                            |
|    6     |  1.525   | Brumazone                   | Outer Parahabitable        |
|    7     |  3.003   | Brumazone                   | Outer Parahabitable        |
|  $H_5$   |  4.045   |                             |                            |
|    8     |  4.739   | Cryozone                    | Xenotic                    |
|    9     |  11.379  | Cryozone                    | Xenotic                    |
|    10    |  18.298  | Cryozone                    | Xenotic                    |
|    11    |  33.357  | Cryozone                    | Xenotic                    |

**Perannual Orbit**

We can calculate the perannual orbital distance and the star's spectral type by:

$$
\begin{aligned}
L &= N^2 = 0.834^2 = 0.696 \\[1ex]
M &= \sqrt[3.8]{L} = \sqrt[3.8]{0.696} = 0.909 \\[1ex]
A &= \sqrt[3]{0.909} = 0.969\;AU\; \checkmark
\end{aligned}
$$

The perannual orbit in this system is at $A = 0.969\;AU$.

Wait: we already know that our nucleal orbit is at $N = 0.834\;AU$. *If* we put planemon on the perannual orbit at $A = 0.969\;AU$ the interval between the nucleal orbit and the perannual orbit is only:

$$
I = \dfrac{0.969}{0.834} = 1.162\;AU\;✓
$$
… which is less than our specified minimum $I > 1.500\;AU$.

Either we don't have a planemon on the nucleal orbit, *or* we don't have a planemon on the perannual orbit; those orbits are fixed by the stellar parameters – neither can be shifted.  This is the power — but also the limitation — of our system. Some things we can tweak as we please; other things we have to work with, or work around.

In this case, we're forced to choose between a planemon with Earth's stellar flux, or a planemon with Earth's orbital period, but we can't have both.  We'll drop the nucleal planemon and go with the parahabitable planemon ... let's work that through. Here's a modified orbit table taking that into account:

| Orbit | Distance | <center>Thermozone</center> | <center>Animozone</center> | Interval |
| :---------------: | :------: | --------------------------- | ------------------------- | -------- |
| 1 | 0.101 | Igniozone | Xenotic | |
| 2 | 0.190 | Igniozone | Xenotic | 1.884 |
| 3 | 0.298 | Ignoizone | Xenotic | 1.573 |
| $H_0$ | 0.417 | | | |
| 4 | 0.482 | Calorozone | Inner Parahabitable | 1.616 |
| $H_1$ | 0.626 | | | |
| $H_2$ | 0.792 | | | |
| 5 ($A$) | 0.969 | Solarazone | Hospitable | »1.927« |
| $H_3$ | 1.115 | | | |
| $H_4$ | 1.476 | | | |
| 6 | 1.525 | Brumazone | Outer Parahabitable | »1.574« |
| 7 | 3.003 | Brumazone | Outer Parahabitable | |
| $H_5$ | 4.045 | | | |
| 8 | 4.739 | Cryozone | Xenotic | 1.552 |
| 9 | 11.379 | Cryozone | Xenotic | 1.608 |
| 10 | 18.298 | Cryozone | Xenotic | 1.823 |
| 11 | 33.357 | Cryozone | Xenotic | 1.778 |

The interval between the perannual orbit and the next closer-in orbit is:

$$
I = \dfrac{0.969}{0.482} = 1.927\;AU
$$

… *just* within our $I = 2.000\;AU$ maximum, and the interval to the next orbit out is:

$$
I = \dfrac{1.525}{0.969} = 1.574\;AU
$$

… which, again, is *just* within our specified minimum $I = 1.500\;AU$.

> [!note]
> Planemons can certainly have wider intervals between their orbits than $I = 2.000\;AU$ – we just never want them to have an interval *less-than* $I = 1.500\;AU$.

And we can calculate that since $A = 0.969\;AU$ and $N = 0.834\;AU$, and we know that the stellar flux at $N = 1.0$, we can calculate that $\Delta$ is:

$$
\Delta = \dfrac{0.969}{0.834} = 1.162
$$

 — which tells us that the perannual orbit is $1.162 \times$ farther out than the nucleal orbit, and since intensity decreases with the square of the distance:

$$
F = \dfrac{1}{\Delta^2}
$$
 
 — we can calculate that the perannual planemon receives:

$$
F = \dfrac{1}{1.162^2}= \dfrac{1}{1.350} = 0.741
$$

… about $74.1$% of the stellar flux as the nucleal orbit does... but that's still:

$$
\begin{aligned}
H_I &= -0.26\dfrac{D}{N} + 1.26 \\[1ex]
&= -0.26\dfrac{0.969}{0.834} + 1.26 \\[1ex]
&= -0.26(1.162) + 1.26 \\[1ex]
&= -0.302 + 1.26 = \boxed{0.958}
\end{aligned}
$$

… an [[(DEPR) 12. Orbital Habitability Index|orbital habitability index]] of $95.8$% that of the nucleal orbit. Slightly cooler, but not drastically so.

# The Planet Eidara

## Physical Parameters

| Parameter         | Description                          | Symbol |
| ----------------- | ------------------------------------ | ------ |
| ⟶ Mass            | $1.05$ t<br>$3.153 \times 10^{-6}$ s | m      |
| ⟶ Radius          | $1.02$ t<br>$6498.42$ km             | r      |
| ⟵ Density         | $0.989$ t<br>$5.453$ g/cm³           | ρ      |
| ⟵ Gravity         | $1.009$ t<br>$9.888$ m/s²            | g      |
| ⟵ Escape Velocity | $1.015$ t<br>$11.37$ km/s            | vₑ     |
## Chronometrics

| Item                     | Description                     | Symbol           |                                                                                   |
| ------------------------ | ------------------------------- | ---------------- | --------------------------------------------------------------------------------- |
| Rotation                 | $1.125_t$<br>$27_h$             | $\delta$ (diurn) |                                                                                   |
| Revolution<br>(sidereal) | $1.125^y$<br>$410.9134_d$       | $\chi$ (chronum) |                                                                                   |
| Revolution<br>(tropical) | $1.12495_y$<br>$410.8987_d$     | $\chi_t$         |                                                                                   |
| Gyrostat                 | Primary Solstice<br>$125^\circ$ | Γ                | Always true anomaly of the Primary Solstice<br>Γ := Λ - 90°                       |
| Chronostat               | Primary Equinox<br>$215^\circ$  | $\Lambda$        | Always true anomaly of the Chronostat<br>(ascending node; onset of Prime Equinox) |
| Sectastat                | $35^\circ$                      | ζ                | Always true anomaly of the first seasonal event occurring after periapsis.        |
| Precession<br>Cycle      | $28994_y$<br>$25772_\chi$       | $\Psi$           |                                                                                   |

# Naked-Eye Visibility
Different sized non-luminous bodies with different albedos are easier or harder to see with the naked eye depending on distance from the illuminating source, the source's brightness, and the distance between the reflecting object and the observer. This is relationship is captured in:

$$
F_{rel} = L\;R^2\left(\frac{A}{A_C}\right)\left(\frac{D_{std}}{D}\right)^4
$$
Where:
- $F_{rel}$ = dimensionless relative apparent flux (brightness ratio)- $R$ = radius of the planet in terrans
- $L$ = luminosity of the star in solar units
- $R$ = radius of the planet in terran units
- $A$ = albedo of the planet
- $A_C$​ = the albedo of the chosen standard candle
	- 0.25 — Terrestrial body
	- 0.48 — Gas giant body
- $D_{std}$ = Naked-eye visibility threshold distance in AU
	- 2 AU — Terrestrial body
	- 19 AU — Gas/ice giant body
- $D$ = Actual observer distance from the planet in AU

### Interpretation

- **$F_{rel} = 1$** → the body is *just visible* to the naked eye at the specified $D_{std}$. 
- **$F_{rel} > 1$** → the body is *brighter* (visible beyond $D_{std}$). 
- **$F_{rel} < 1$** → the body is *fainter* (would need to be closer to be seen). 
- The **$L$** term scales with the *incident stellar flux*, so worlds around dimmer stars appear proportionally fainter, and those around brighter stars proportionally brighter. 
- The **$D^{-4}$** term captures both inverse-square effects: 
 - one from the **illumination** of the planet by its star ($1/D^2$), and 
 - one from the **reflected light** spreading out toward the observer ($1/D^2$ again). 
- Together, these define a simple, dimensionless measure of apparent brightness relative to a chosen *standard candle*.

## Planetary Albedo Estimations

| Surface Type / Modifier   | <center>Base Albedo Estimate (A)</center> | Notes                                                 |
| :------------------------ | ----------------------------------------: | :---------------------------------------------------- |
| Fresh snow/ice            |                                       0.8 | Highly reflective; contributes to snowball effect     |
| Old snow / glacial crust  |                                       0.6 | Still reflective but more absorptive than fresh snow  |
| Desert (sand)             |                                       0.3 | Can vary with mineral content and compaction          |
| Grassland                 |                                       0.2 | Moderate reflectivity; varies with seasonal dryness   |
| Forest (deciduous)        |                                      0.15 | Dark under canopy; absorbs most light                 |
| Forest (coniferous)       |                                      0.13 | Darker needles + canopy structure reduce reflectivity |
| Rocky surface             |                                      0.18 | Can vary widely depending on coloration and texture   |
| Ocean (high sun angle)    |                                      0.06 | Absorbs most sunlight when directly overhead          |
| Ocean (low sun angle)     |                                       0.2 | Reflects more at shallow angles; up to 0.20 or more   |
| Dense clouds (Venus-like) |                                      0.75 | Thick sulfuric clouds like on Venus; extremely bright |
| Thin clouds (Earth-like)  |                                      0.35 | Cloud cover over oceans or land increases albedo      |
| No clouds / clear sky     |                                      0.05 | Near-minimal reflectivity, especially over ocean      |

## Naked-Eye Albedo Scaling for Giant Planets

This is based on the fact that a Jupiter-sized planet becomes too dim to see with the naked eye at distances farther than $19$ AU for a star with the Sun's Luminosity ($L⊙$). For stars with luminosities different than the Sun's, $L$ is expressed in solar units.

$$
\begin{array}{ll}
D_{eye} = 19\;\sqrt{\dfrac{R}{11}}\;\sqrt[4]{\dfrac{A\;L}{0.48\;F_{rel}}} &\text{Brighter albedo} \\[1em]
D_{eye} = 19\;\sqrt{\dfrac{R}{11}}\;\sqrt[4]{\dfrac{0.48\;F_{rel}}{A\;L}} &\text{Darker albedo}
\end{array}
$$

Where:
- $D_{eye}$ = maximum distance in AU of naked-eye visibility
- $A$ = albedo of planet
- $L$ = luminosity of the star in solar units
- $R$ = radius of planet in terrans

## Naked-Eye Albedo Scaling for Terrestrial Planets
This is based on the fact that an Earth-sized planet becomes too dim to see with the naked eye at distances farther than $2$ AU for a star with the Sun's Luminosity ($L⊙$). For stars with luminosities different than the Sun's, $L$ is expressed in solar units.

$$
\begin{array}{ll}
D_{eye} = 2\;\sqrt{R}\;\sqrt[4]{\dfrac{A\;L}{0.25\;F_{rel}}} & \text{Brighter albedo} \\[1em]
D_{eye} = 2\;\sqrt{R}\;\sqrt[4]{\dfrac{0.25\;F_{rel}}{A\;L}} & \text{Darker albedo}
\end{array}
$$

Where:
- $D_{eye}$ = maximum distance in AU of naked-eye visibility
- $A$ = albedo of planet
- $L$ = luminosity of the star in solar units
- $R$ = radius of planet in terrans

These relationships allow direct comparison of visibility distances for worlds of different size, albedo, and host-star luminosity under the same visual threshold $F_{rel}$​.
