## The Small Angle Approximation
When the apparent angular size ($\theta$) of an object (or the distance between two objects) in the sky is small — typically less than 10° — the sine and tangent of $\theta$ are nearly equal to $\theta$ itself when measured in radians:

$$
\theta ≈ \frac{d}{D} \times \frac{180}{\pi} = 57.2958\;\frac{d}{D}
$$
Where:
- $\theta$ = angular diameter (in degrees)
- $d$ = true diameter of the object (or separation between the objects)
- $D$ = distance to the object in the same units as $d$

When working in radians:

$$
\theta^c = \frac{d}{D}
$$
Where:
- $\theta^c$ = angular diameter (in radianss)
- $d$ = true diameter of the object (or separation between the objects)
- $D$ = distance to the object in the same units as $d$

## Measuring Exactly
If one wants to be precise, the appropriate equation is:

$$
\theta^c = 2\;arctan\;\left(\frac{d}{2D}\right)
$$
Where:
- $\theta^c$ = angular diameter (in radianss)
- $d$ = true diameter of the object (or separation between the objects)
- $D$ = distance to the object in the same units as $d$

To get the angle in degrees, convert:

$$
\begin{array}{l}
\theta^\circ = 2\;arctan\;\left(\dfrac{d}{2D}\right) \times \dfrac{180}{\pi} \\
\theta^\circ = 2\;arctan\;\left(\dfrac{d}{2D}\right) \times 57.2958
\end{array}
$$
## Approximating Angular Sizes (or Separations)
But, how does one arrive at an angular measure in the first place?  Well, there's this thing called a sextant....

Actually, there's a much simpler method using an instrument almost everyone has with them at all times — the hand at the end of their arm.  When the arm is fully extended, the hand (and its various parts) form a remarkably consistent angular ruler:

| Gesture                                              | Approximate<br>Angle | Notes                                                |
| ---------------------------------------------------- | -------------------- | ---------------------------------------------------- |
| Width of little finger                               | ≈ 1°                 | about twice the apparent diameter of the Sun or Moon |
| Width of first three fingers                         | ≈ 15°                | a convenient "finder field" in binoculars            |
| Width of closed fist                                 | ≈ 10°                | one _decadal_ unit — useful for quick sky hops       |
| Span from thumb to little finger ("rock on" gesture) | ≈ 20 – 25°           | a coarse measure for constellation spans             |
The most use a worldmaker will find for this trick is when you have calculated the size in degrees of something in your world's sky (or the separation between two objects in the heavens), you can get a sense for how "really" big that would appear by holding out your hand and approximating the appropriate angular measure.

The most use a worldmaker will find for this trick is when you have calculated the size in degrees of something in your world's sky (or the separation between two objects in the heavens), you can get a sense for how "really" big that would appear by holding out your hand and approximating the appropriate angular measure.

For instance; say you've calculated that your world's sun is about $3^\circ$ in the sky... that's about the width of the first two fingers held up at arm's length.

This is essentially the **practical inverse** of the *small-angle approximation* discussed earlier.

#### Universally Applicable
Because hand and arm proportions scale together with age and body size, this method works for nearly everyone — from children to giants — with only slight variations. And since this is an *approximate* method to begin with, those small differences can be safely ignored.

## The Synodial Epoch

The *synodial epoch* ($Y$) is the time interval after which a synodion occurs at the base angle $B^\theta$ agin, completing a full cycle.

The *epochal aggregate* ($Y_0$) is the total number of synodia that transpire within a synodial epoch.

Whereas we used the synodic formula:

$$
\begin{aligned}
\Sigma &= \dfrac{P_\alpha \times P_\beta}{|P_\alpha - P_\beta|} \\
\end{aligned}
$$
to calculate the synodic period, we need to use a different method to calculate the Epochal Aggregate; we employ both the *Least Common Multiple* (LCM) and the *Greatest Common Divisor* (GCD):

$$
LCM(a, b) = \dfrac{a \times b}{GCD(a, b)}
$$
>❗️Important ❗️
>1. Most modern calculators, spreadsheets, and programming languages have built-in functions for calculating both LCM and GCD, but be aware that *both of these can only be correctly calculated for integer inputs*.  If something like LCM(1.5, 3.3) returns a value rather than an error, it will likely be the LCM of 1 and 3, not of 1.5 and 3.3.
>2. The GCD *can* be calculated by hand using the *Euclidean Algorithm* (detailed elsewhere), calculators, spreadsheets, or programmed scripts are much faster.

We need to calculate the LCM of $P_\alpha = 24.36$ and $P_\beta = 11.72$, but neither of these are integers, so we *normalize* them by multiplying by the factor of ten which will render the more precise of the two into an integer value.  In this case, both have two decimal places of precision, so multiplying by $10^2 = 100$ will be sufficient:

$$
P'_\alpha = 24.36 \times 100 = 2436 \quad \text{and} \quad P'_\beta = 11.72 \times 100 = 1172
$$
Now we can compute the LCM by:

$$
\begin{aligned}
\begin{split}
LCM(a, b) &= \dfrac{a \times b}{GCD(a, b)} \\[0.5em]
&= \dfrac{2436 \times 1172}{GCD(2436, 1172)} \\[0.5em]
&= \dfrac{2854992}{4} \\
&= 713748\; ✓
\end{split}
\end{aligned}
$$
Now, *since we normalized our inputs* by scaling up by a factor of $10^2 = 100$, we need to scale this result *down* by the same factor:

$$
Y_0 = \dfrac{713748}{100} = 7137.48\; \text{days } ✓
$$
The *epochal interval* ($\Psi$) is calculated by dividing the Epochal Aggregate ($Y_0$) by the synodic period ($\Sigma$):

$$
\begin{aligned}
\begin{split}
\Psi &= \dfrac{Y_0}{\Sigma} \\[0.5em]
&= \dfrac{7137.48}{22.587} \\[0.5em]
&= 316\; \text{synodia}
\end{split}
\end{aligned}
$$
This reveals that $T_{316}$ occurs in the same configuration at the base angle $B^\theta$.  We can double-check by supplying $S=316$ to our synodian instance angle ($\widehat{\Theta}$) equation:

$$
\begin{aligned}
\begin{split}
\widehat{\Theta} &= 360^\circ - MOD((S \times \Lambda_\Theta), 360^\circ) \\
&= 360^\circ - MOD((316 \times 26.203^\circ), 360^\circ) \\
&= 360^\circ - 360^\circ \\
&= 0\; ✓
\end{split}
\end{aligned}
$$
To convert the epochal aggregate $\Psi = 7137.48$ days into something more useful, we can divide by the number of days in a sidereal year ($≈ 365.2564$):

$$
Y_0^y = \dfrac{7137.48}{365.2564} = 19.54\; \text{years }✓
$$
… which is about

$$
19^y\;197^d\;11^h\;16^m\;20.724^s
$$
give-or-take a millisecond.

### Quarter Synodial Epoch

#### The Where
A quarter synodial epoch ($Y_{/4}$) is an important quantity, as well; at these intervals, synodoi occur precisely on a cardinal angle ($90^\circ, 180^\circ, 270^\circ$) in succession.  We call these the *cardinal synodoi*.  They occur, of course every one fourth of the synodion:

$$
Y_{/4} = \dfrac{Y_0}{4}
$$
In our worked example above, the quarter synodial epoch is:

$$
\begin{aligned}
Y_{/4} = \dfrac{Y_0}{4} = \dfrac{7137.48}{4} = 1784.37\; \text{days} \\[0.5em]
Y_{/4}^y = \dfrac{Y_{/4}}{365.2564} = \dfrac{1784.37}{365.2564} ≈ 4.89\; \text{years}
\end{aligned}
$$
### Quarter Epochal Interval
Similarly, a *quarter epochal interval* ($\Psi_{/4}$) is the number of *common synodoi* that occur between cardinal synodoi:

$$
\Psi_{/4} = \dfrac{\Psi_0}{4} = \dfrac{316}{4} = 79\; \text{synodoi}
$$
### The Epochal Complements
There are two of these, the *epochal major complement* ($Y_\alpha$) and the *epochal minor complement* ($Y_\beta$), which are the number of complete orbits of orbiton $M_\alpha$ and $M_\beta$, respectively, per each synodial epoch:

$$
\begin{aligned}
Y_\alpha &= \dfrac{Y_0}{P_\alpha} \\[1em]
Y_\beta &= \dfrac{Y_0}{P_\beta}
\end{aligned}
$$
In our worked example above:

$$
\begin{aligned}
Y_\alpha &= \dfrac{Y_0}{P_\alpha} = \dfrac{7137.48}{24.36} = 293\; \text{orbits }✓ \\[1em]
Y_\beta &= \dfrac{Y_0}{P_\beta} = \dfrac{7137.48}{11.72} = 609\; \text{orbits }✓
\end{aligned}
$$

[^1]: While the notation ²/₁ is notationally equivalent to 2 : 1, we use the colon format exclusively.

[^2]: literally, "having a common measure"; the orbit of one can't be expressed by a whole number or rational fraction of the other's.

# The Terric Domain

## What the Terric Domain Is

The **Terric Domain** defines the class of **mesanthropic** worlds within the **Biotic Order**:  
COH-amegic environments whose physical structure is **potentially survivable for humans**, given moderate technological support.

Terric worlds are not required to be Earthlike.  
They are required to be **human-adjacent**.

A Terric world supports:

- COH-animosys in principle    
- long-term surface or subsurface stability    
- gravity, pressure, and chemistry within broad human tolerances    

In short:

> **Terric worlds are those where humans can plausibly survive without rewriting their biology.**

## Axis Placement (Explicit and Non-Negotiable)

The Terric Domain is defined by the **intersection** of two axes:

- **Amegic Axis:** COH-amegic (inherited from Biotic)    
- **Anthropic Axis:** mesanthropic    

It does **not** introduce a new substrate, nor does it define morphology.

- **Morphotic Axis:** typically duromonic, but not defined here    

This matters.  The Terric Domain does **not** describe:

- ecological richness    
- climate stability    
- biospheric maturity    
- comfort or convenience    

Those are downstream refinements.
## What “Terric” Means (and What It Doesn’t)

**Terric means “structurally compatible with human survival.”**

It does _not_ mean:

- breathable without assistance    
- temperate    
- biologically rich    
- politically or narratively convenient    

A Terric world may be:

- hostile at the surface    
- thin-aired or dense-aired    
- geologically young or unstable    
- biologically sparse or sterile    

Yet still remain Terric if its **physical envelope** permits sustained human presence with engineering rather than transformation.

> **Terric is about survivability, not comfort.**
## Terric as the Human-Adjacent Envelope

Within the Biotic Order, the Terric Domain represents the **widest physically meaningful range** of worlds that humans can interact with directly.

This is the zone where:

- pressure vessels suffice    
- habitats are plausible    
- terraforming is meaningful    
- long-term colonization is imaginable    

Below Terric, human presence becomes transient or impossible.  
Above Terric, additional constraints narrow the field.

Terric is therefore the **workhorse category** of human-facing worldbuilding.
## Relation to Other Animotic Classes

The Terric Domain is a **subset** of the Biotic Order and a **superset** of more restrictive anthropic classes.

- **Biotic Order** — COH-animosys possible    
- **Terric Domain** — human survival possible    
- **Geotic Realm** — human habitation broadly viable    
- **Gaean Province** — human comfort native    

All Geotics and Gaeans are Terric.  
Most Terrics are neither.

This asymmetry is intentional.
## Terric vs. Kybelic (Critical Distinction)

The Terric Domain is **anthropic**.  
The **Kybelic Domain** is **amegic**.

Kybelic worlds:

- are optimized for COH-animosys productivity    
- often exceed human gravity, pressure, or metabolic tolerance    
- may host extraordinarily rich biospheres    

Terric worlds:

- prioritize human compatibility    
- may sacrifice biospheric density for survivability    

Kybelics are **animotically lateral**, not anthropically nested.
## Time Matters: Terric Is a State, Not a Destiny

Like all animotic classifications, Terric is **state-based**.

A world may:

- enter the Terric Domain as atmospheres thicken    
- leave it as gravity increases or volatiles are lost    
- pass through Terric briefly on the way to Geotic    
- fall back out due to climate collapse or stellar evolution    

Terric is a **window**, not a guarantee.
## Why the Terric Domain Exists in Wimby

Worldbuilders need a way to say:

> “Humans can live here — but it won’t be easy.”

The Terric Domain provides that answer without:

- conflating survivability with habitability   
- collapsing engineering into biology    
- assuming Earth norms by default    

It is the **minimum anthropic threshold** above Biotic worlds and the **maximum design freedom** below Geotic ones.
## Relationship to Planemonics (Explicit but Secondary)

The Terric Domain is **not defined by equations**.

Certain ranges of:

- mass    
- gravity    
- density    
- escape velocity    

**correlate strongly** with Terric survivability, while others exclude it outright. These relationships are discussed where they belong: in **planemonic guidance**, not animotic definition.

> Terric defines _who can survive_.  
> Planemonics constrains _where that survival is plausible_.

The arrow only points one way.

## Summary (for Fast Readers)

- **Terric = mesanthropic Biotic worlds**    
- COH-animosys is possible, and humans can survive with support    
- Terric does not imply comfort, stability, or richness    
- It is the broadest human-adjacent envelope    
- All Geotics and Gaeans are Terric; most Terrics are neither    
- Kybelics are lateral, not nested    
- Terric is state-based and historically contingent    

If a world is Terric, humans can plausibly live there.  
Whether they _want_ to is another question entirely.

# The Xenotic Order

## What the Xenotic Order Is

The **Xenotic Order** defines the class of worlds whose animosys potential lies **outside the carboxyhydroxyl (COH) biochemical regime**.

Xenotic worlds may support:

- non-COH chemical lineages    
- radically non-Terran metabolic cycles    
- animosys operating under substrates, solvents, or energetics alien to Earth biology    

They are **not defined by size, mass, or appearance**, but by **animochemical divergence**.

In short:

> **Xenotic worlds are those where animosys may arise — but not in ways Earth biology can recognize as its own.**
## Axis Placement (Explicit and Non-Negotiable)

The Xenotic Order is defined **exclusively** on the **Amegic Axis**.

- **Amegic Axis:** non-COH amegic    
- **Anthropic Axis:** _not applicable_    
- **Morphotic Axis:** _not specified_    

This matters.

Xenotic classification does **not** describe:

- habitability for humans    
- surface comfort    
- planetary familiarity    
- narrative strangeness    

It describes **substrate incompatibility**.
## What “Xenotic” Means (and What It Doesn’t)

**Xenotic means “chemically alien to COH-animosys.”**

It does _not_ mean:

- physically extreme    
- visually strange    
- hostile by default    
- rare by necessity    

A Xenotic world may be:

- Earth-sized    
- gravity-normal    
- atmospherically thick    
- thermally moderate    

…and still be Xenotic if its animosys depends on **non-COH chemistry**.

> **Xenotic is about substrate, not spectacle.**
## Xenotic as the Non-COH Branch of Animosys

Within the animotic framework, Xenotics represent the **parallel evolutionary space** to Biotic worlds.

Where Biotic worlds explore:

- carbon–oxygen–hydrogen chemistry    
- water-dominated solvents    
- Earth-adjacent energetics    

Xenotic worlds explore:

- alternative solvents (ammonia, methane, supercritical fluids)    
- non-carbon backbones or hybrid lattices    
- exotic redox cycles and energy gradients    

These lineages are **lateral**, not inferior or superior.
## Relation to Other Animotic Classes

The Xenotic Order is **not nested** within the Biotic Order.

- **Biotic Order** — COH-animosys possible    
- **Xenotic Order** — non-COH animosys possible    

A world belongs to **one or the other**, never both.

Physical similarity does not override chemical divergence.
## Xenotic vs. Biotic (Critical Boundary)

The boundary between Biotic and Xenotic is **sharp**.

If a world’s animosys:

- relies on carbon backbones    
- uses oxygen-mediated energetics    
- operates in hydrogen-dominated solvents    

…it is **Biotic**, regardless of how strange its ecosystem appears.

If it does not, it is **Xenotic**, even if the planet is Earth-sized and gravity-normal.

> **Substrate draws the line. Everything else is secondary.**
## Time Matters: Xenotic Is a State, Not an Essence

Like all animotic classifications, Xenotic is **state-based**.

A world may:

- transition from Xenotic to Biotic as chemistry shifts    
- host multiple animochemical phases across its history    
- lose Xenotic potential through thermal or chemical change    

Xenotic status reflects **current animochemical viability**, not permanence.
## Why the Xenotic Order Exists in Wimby

Worldbuilders need a way to say:

> “Life exists here — but it is not Earthlife, and Earth assumptions do not apply.”

The Xenotic Order provides that answer without:

- anthropocentric bias    
- numerical gatekeeping    
- aesthetic exoticism    

It preserves the possibility of **true biological otherness** without collapsing into fantasy.
## Relationship to Planemonics (Explicit but Secondary)

The Xenotic Order is **not defined by physical ranges**.

Xenotic animosys may arise in:

- rocky worlds   
- icy bodies    
- gas giants    
- deep atmospheres    
- subsurface oceans    

Planemonics constrain **where specific xenotic chemistries are plausible**, but do not define Xenotic status itself.

> Xenotic defines _what kind of animosys is possible_.  
> Planemonics constrain _which bodies can host it_.

The arrow only points one way.
## Summary (for Fast Readers)

- **Xenotic = non-COH animosys is possible**    
- It is an **amegic classification**, not an anthropic one    
- Physical similarity to Earth is irrelevant    
- Xenotic worlds are chemically alien, not merely strange    
- Xenotic is lateral to Biotic, not nested within it    
- Xenotic status is state-based and historically contingent    

If a world is Xenotic, Earth biology provides no reliable guide.  
Something alive may be there — but it will be **other**.

 # 🚫 When *Not* to Use GEWE

[[The GEWE Corollary]] is powerful — but misused, it becomes an excuse for sloppy craft.  
There are times when bending the rules weakens the world instead of strengthening it.

Use GEWE **wisely**, not ***whenever***.

## ❌ 1. When Consistency Is the Point
If your story hinges on the reliability of:

- a magic system  
- a technology  
- a cosmology  
- an ecology  
- a political structure  

…then violating its own rules breaks trust.

Readers don’t mind a flexible world.  
They hate an *incoherent* one — Douglas Adams and Terry Pratchett, the patron saints of absurdity, played by rules they never broke.  Theirs are examples worthy of following.

GEWE is for bending, not reneging.

## ❌ 2. When the Correction Is Easy
If the problem can be fixed by:

- tweaking a number,  
- renaming a metal,  
- nudging an orbit,  
- moving a mountain,  
- adjusting a timeline,  

…then do the fix.

GEWE is not for avoiding small, painless edits.

## ❌ 3. When It Creates a Bigger Problem Later
A cool exception today can become a continuity nightmare tomorrow.

Examples:

- Making the sun “a tiny bit magic”  
- Letting time-travel work one time because it’s convenient  
- Letting a species break its own biology for a single scene  

GEWE is not a patch over a load-bearing beam.

## ❌ 4. When It Breaks Reader Expectations
If the genre, tone, or genre-contract demands internal rigor:

- hard SF  
- tightly constructed mysteries  
- political thrillers  
- stories about engineers/scientists  
- grimdark survival tales  

…then GEWE can feel like cheating.

Even fantasy readers expect **internal logic**, even if the logic is magical.

## ❌ 5. When It Undermines a Character or Theme
If the exception:

- invalidates a sacrifice  
- erases a consequence  
- contradicts a theme  
- weakens character agency  

…don’t use GEWE.

Story coherence always outranks convenience.

## ❌ 6. When It Becomes a Habit
If “GEWE will save me” becomes your plan,  
you’re no longer bending rules — you’re relying on them breaking.

That’s the opposite of worldmaking integrity.

---
# ✔ The Litmus Test

Before invoking GEWE, ask:

**“Does this bend deepen the story, or just avoid work?”**

If it deepens the story → GEWE approved.  
If it just avoids work → revise the world.

GEWE is permission, not permission *slipshod*.

# Final Words
All the above being said… if you trim your planet’s “month” to a neat 32.5 days instead of what you calculated as 32.60237 days, and you’re fairly sure no one is going to sit down with a calculator to catch you out...?

_Mundus tuum est_.

[[The GEWE Corollary]]
[[The SANC Charter]]


🔝 [[01. What is WMB?]]

# The Varymonic  Mass Series

All physical bodies (_monons_) in the WMB framework are described along two orthogonal axes:
- their **Varymonic Series**, which defines the degree of _gravity-dominated_ self-coherence, and
- their **Morphological Character**, which defines material composition and structure.

Monons first divide into **Synomons** (cohesion-bound) and **Varymons** (gravity-bound).

Only _varymons_ admit Varymonic Series distinctions, because these classes arise from **gravitational self-organization**, **compression physics**, and **fusion thresholds**.

At the broadest gravitational scale, all **varymons** fall into one of four Varymonic Series:
- **Duromons** — all varymons with masses below the degenerate matter threshold ($< 5$ j).  
- **Mesomons** — all varymons with masses above $≥ 5$ j and < $80$ j ($0.08$ s)
- **Fusomons** — all varymons with masses in the stable hydrogen-fusion range ($0.08 ·· 300$ s).  
    Subdivided by the **stellar spectral-class system**, which defines their thermodynamic and photometric behavior.    
- **Peromons** — all varymons whose masses exceed the upper stellar-stability limit ($> 300$ s).  
    These occupy the **trans-stellar regime** of radiative instability, pair-creation collapse, or exotic compact states.
## **Varymonic Axis**

_Defines gravitational scale and self-coherence — how strongly a body’s gravity shapes its structure._

- Applies **only to varymons**    
- Ranges from micromons to hypermons    
- Reflects internal pressure, compression, fusion onset, and relativistic collapse    
- Field: **Mononics**
## **Morphotic Axis

_Defines material/structural composition — what the body is made of._

This is covered in a separate series of articles.
# 2. Varymon Series

_All gravitationally-coherent monons_

This superclass contains four major subclasses, distinguished by the dominant internal physics shaping each regime:

- **Duromons** — classical-matter varymons  
- **Mesomons** — partially degenerate varymons  
- **Fusomons** — fusion-regime varymons  
- **Peromons** — relativistic/exotic varymons
## 2.1. Duromons
### _Matter-regime monons ($0.0018$ t → $1590$ t)_

Bodies whose structure is dominated by **classical matter physics**:
- density    
- composition    
- radius    
- phase state    
- hydrostatic self-cohesion    
- escape velocity    
- non-fusion thermodynamics
    
**Duromons are where Animotics most commonly manifest**, because only classical-matter regimes support stable chemical and ecological substrates.
## 2.2. Mesomons ($1590$ t → $4131$ t; $5$ j → $13$ j)

This superclass includes:
- transimons
	- electron-degenerate bodies with constant radius
- intermons    
	- deuterium-fusors    
	- non-hydrogenic fusors
	    - the entire L–T–Y dwarf continuum
    
Mesomons are the “liminal monons,” bridging duromons and fusomons.
### _Transimon ($1590$ t → $4131$ t; $5$ j → $13$ j)_

Bodies bridging planetary and brown-dwarf physics, dominated by:
- electron degeneracy pressure,    
- deep convective envelopes,    
### _Intermons ($13$ j → $80$ j)_

Bodies bridging planetary and stellar physics, dominated by:
- partial fusion,    
- deep convective envelopes,    
- brown-dwarf thermodynamics,    
- and non-hydrogen catalytic fusion episodes.
    
They “borrow rules” from both duromonic and fusomonic regimes.
## 2.3. Fusomons
### _Stellar monons ($80$ j → ~$300$ s)_

Bodies defined by **sustained core hydrogen fusion**, including:
- main-sequence stars    
- pre-/post-main-sequence fusors    
- spectral-class morphotypes    
- luminosity-cluster somatypes    
- variability families    

The fusomon system already has:
- spectral classes    
- luminosity classes    
- ontozones    
- perannual & nucleal orbit mapping    
- Solar Analogs/Cognates/Twins
    
This is the most "borrowed-in" subsystem in WMB (with some tweaks).
## 2.4. Peromons
### _Ultra-/Supermassive monons (> $10$ megaterran)_

Bodies whose structure is dominated by:
- relativity    
- spacetime curvature    
- degenerate pressure    
- accretion physics    
- relativistic mass aggregation    
- event-horizon dynamics    

Including:
- supramons (stellar-mass black holes and neutron stars)    
- ultramons supermassive black holes (SMBHs)    
- hypermons (hypercollapses, primordial monsters, etc.)    

Peromons complete the varymonic mass spectrum: the final extreme for independently self-coherent bodies.

![[Varymon_Series_Mass_Domains.svg]]

$$
\begin{array}{l r r c l}
\textbf{Series} 
& \textbf{Min. Mass} 
& \textbf{Max. Mass}
& \textbf{Range}\;( \,t) \\
\hline
\text{hypermon}  & 3,000,000,000\,s & 3,000,000,000,000\,s & (10^{15} \cdot\cdot\; 10^{18})  \\
\text{ultramon}  & 3,000,000\,s & 3,000,000,000\,s & (10^{12} \cdot\cdot\; 10^{15})  \\
\text{supramon}  & 10,000,000 \,t~(300\,s) & 3,000,000\,s & (10^{7} \cdot\cdot\; 10^{12})  \\
\hline
\text{stellamon} & 25,308 \,t~(80\;j) & 10,000,000 \,t~(300\,s) & (10^{5} \cdot\cdot\; 10^{7})  \\
\hline
\text{intermon}  & 4,131 \,t~(13\;j)         & 25,308 \,t~(80\;j) & (10^{3} \cdot\cdot\; 10^{5})  \\
\text{transimon}  & 1590 \,t~(5\;j)         & 4131 \,t~(80\;j) & (10^{3})  \\
\hline
\textbf{planemon} & 0.01 \,t        & 1590 \,t~(5\;j) & (10^{-2} \cdot\cdot\; 10^{3})  \\
\text{metamon}   & 0.001 \,t        & 0.01 \,t     & (10^{-3} \cdot\cdot\; 10^{-2})  \\
\text{midimon}   & 0.0001 \,t      & 0.001 \,t   & (10^{-4} \cdot\cdot\; 10^{-3}) \\
\text{minimon}  & 0.00001 \,t     & 0.0001 \,t  & (10^{-5} \cdot\cdot\; 10^{-4}) & \\
\text{micromon}  & 0.000001 \,t     & 0.00001 \,t  & (10^{-6} \cdot\cdot\; 10^{-5}) \\
\end{array}
$$

# Introduction

The following convention establishes how **Worldmaking Basics (WMB)** extends the existing SI metric prefixes to allow for discussion of ranges that are specific to a particular order of magnitude, rather than resorting to compounds.  For instance:
	100 micro- = 1 demi-
 — and so forth.

These prefixes cover all of the existing SI prefixes, which are retained exactly as officially defined.  This extension ensures that both scientific notation and symbolic terminology remain intuitive across a vast range of scales.
## Prefixes
$$
\begin{array}{l c r l}
\textbf{Intervals} &
\textbf{Abbr.} &
\textbf{Power} \\[3pt]
\hline
\text{quecto-}  & \text{q} & 10^{-30} \\
\textit{centironto-}  & \text{c-q} & 10^{-29} \\
\textit{decironto-}  & \text{d-q} & 10^{-28} \\
\text{ronto-}  & \text{r} & 10^{-27} \\
\textit{centiyocto-}  & \text{c-y} & 10^{-26} \\
\textit{deciyocto-}  & \text{d-y} & 10^{-25} \\
\text{yocto-}  & \text{y} & 10^{-24} \\
\textit{centizepto-}  & \text{c-z} & 10^{-23} \\
\textit{decizepto-}  & \text{d-z} & 10^{-22} \\
\text{zepto-}  & \text{z} & 10^{-21} \\
\textit{centiatto-}  & \text{c-a} & 10^{-20} \\
\textit{deciatto-}  & \text{d-a} & 10^{-19} \\
\text{atto-}  & \text{a} & 10^{-18} \\
\textit{centifemto-}  & \text{c-f} & 10^{-17} \\
\textit{decifemto-}  & \text{d-f} & 10^{-16} \\
\text{femto-}  & \text{f} & 10^{-15} \\
\textit{centipico-}  & \text{c-p} & 10^{-14} \\
\textit{decipico-}  & \text{d-p} & 10^{-13} \\
\text{pico-}  & \text{p} & 10^{-12} \\
\textbf{lilli-}  & \text{ll} & 10^{-11} \\
\textbf{iota-}  & \text{io} & 10^{-10} \\
\text{nano-}  & \text{n}  & 10^{-9}  \\
\textbf{okti-}  & \text{ok} & 10^{-8}  \\
\textbf{septi-} & \text{sp} & 10^{-7}  \\
\text{micro-} & \mu      & 10^{-6}  \\
\textbf{penti-} & \text{pn} & 10^{-5}  \\
\textbf{demi-}  & \text{dm} & 10^{-4}  \\
\text{milli-} & \text{m}  & 10^{-3}  \\
\text{centi-} & \text{c}  & 10^{-2}  \\
\text{deci-}  & \text{d}  & 10^{-1}  \\
\text{-}      & \text{ }  & 10^{0}   \\
\text{deka-}  & \text{da} & 10^{1}   \\
\text{hecto-} & \text{h}  & 10^{2}   \\
\text{kilo-}  & \text{k}  & 10^{3}   \\
\textbf{myria-} & \text{Mr} & 10^{4}   \\
\text{quinta-}  & \text{Qn} & 10^{5}   \\
\text{mega-}  & \text{M} & 10^{6}   \\
\textbf{hepta-} & \text{Hp} & 10^{7}   \\
\textbf{octa-}  & \text{Oc} & 10^{8}   \\
\text{giga-}  & \text{G} & 10^{9}   \\
\textbf{dena-}  & \text{Dn} & 10^{10}  \\
\textbf{onda-}  & \text{On} & 10^{11}  \\
\text{tera-}  & \text{T}  & 10^{12} \\
\textbf{triska-}& \text{Tr} & 10^{13} \\
\textbf{quadra-}& \text{Qd} & 10^{14} \\
\text{peta-}  & \text{P} & 10^{15} \\
\textbf{sexa-}  & \text{Sx} & 10^{16} \\
\textbf{septa-} & \text{St} & 10^{17} \\
\text{exa-}   & \text{E} & 10^{18} \\
\textbf{brobdi-}   & \text{Bd} & 10^{19} \\
\textbf{tita-}   & \text{Tt} & 10^{20} \\
\text{zetta-}   & \text{Z} & 10^{21} \\
\textit{dekazetta-}   & \text{D-Z} & 10^{22} \\
\textit{hectozetta-}   & \text{H-Z} & 10^{23} \\
\text{yotta-}   & \text{Y} & 10^{24} \\
\textit{dekayotta-}   & \text{D-Y} & 10^{25} \\
\textit{hectoyotta-}   & \text{H-Y} & 10^{26} \\
\text{ronna-}   & \text{R} & 10^{27} \\
\textit{dekaronna-}   & \text{D-R} & 10^{28} \\
\textit{hectoronna-}   & \text{H-R} & 10^{29} \\
\text{quetta-}   & \text{Q} & 10^{30} \\
\end{array}
$$
>Note: Prefixes in *italics* are **WMB**-invented compounds, not official SI prefixes, and thus do not have defined abbreviations; **WMB** uses hyphenated abbreviations solely for completeness.

- Where official SI prefixes exist (e.g., *micro-*, *milli-*, etc.), **WMB** uses them directly (inconsistent in case though they may be).
- These changes allow for all upper-case initials for >10 units and all lower-case initials for all <1 units.
- For intermediate powers without SI prefixes, **WMB** adopts consistent **neologisms** based on the exponent’s magnitude.
- While SI defines prefixes beyond *exa-* ([Metric prefix](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Metric_prefix)), **WMB** rarely uses them in monon contexts.  
   - Even the observable universe spans only ≈ $46.5$ **giga**light-years (Gly) in radius — roughly three orders of magnitude below **teraterran** relevance.  
- The **negative intervals** are symbolically essential: many moons, asteroids, and micro-monons fall far below $1♁$ in mass.  
   - It is often **clearer** to say  
   - “The mass is $1$ microterran $(\mu♁)$”  than  “The mass is $0.000001♁$.”  
- For practical purposes, **WMB** defines the upper bound of each mass interval as **one demiterran** ($10^{-5}; 0.00001♁$) below the next power of ten.  
   - Thus, the *terran* mass interval **technically spans** 
     $1.0♁ ≤ M < 9.99999♁$.  
   - Any value exceeding that threshold — even slightly — **rounds up** to the next symbolic interval.  
     - **Example:**
        $9.999 99 1♁ → 10.0♁$,
        assigning the body to the **dekaterran** interval.  
- The **deniterran** marks the lower limit of the scale; bodies smaller than this cannot achieve hydrostatic equilibrium.  
- The **exaterran** marks the upper limit, approximating the realistic mass ceiling for Ultra-massive Black Holes (UMBHs), about $10^{12}\,M⊙$ ([Supermassive_black_hole](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Supermassive_black_hole#Description)).
## The Micro-unit Subscale

Because many objects are very small in size or in mass — **micromons** have masses best expressed as a **millionth of a terran** ($\mu♁$) — , WMB defines an optional *micro~unit subscale* for intuitive symbolic modeling.

This subscale maps cleanly to the standard symbolic intervals and absolute powers of ten.

$$
\begin{array}{l r r l r r r}
\textbf{$\mu$-Terran Scale} &
\textbf{Min. Mass} &
\textbf{Max. Mass} &
\textbf{Std. Scale} &
\textbf{Power} \\[3pt]
\hline\hline
\text{demimicro- (dm$\mu$)}  & 0.0001 & 0.001  & \text{deni-}  & 10^{-10} \\
\text{millimicro- (m$\mu$)}  & 0.001  & 0.01   & \text{nano-}  & 10^{-9}  \\
\text{centimicro- (c$\mu$)}  & 0.01   & 0.1    & \text{okti-}  & 10^{-8}  \\
\text{decimicro- (d$\mu$)}   & 0.1    & 1      & \text{septi-} & 10^{-7}  \\
\text{micro- ($\mu$)}  & 1      & 10     & \text{micro-} & 10^{-6}  \\
\text{dekamicro- (da$\mu$)}  & 10     & 100    & \text{penti-} & 10^{-5}  \\
\text{hectomicro- (h$\mu$)}  & 100    & 1000   & \text{demi-}  & 10^{-4}  \\
\text{kilomicro- (k$\mu$)}   & 1000   & 10000  & \text{milli-} & 10^{-3}  \\
\text{myriamicro- (Mr$\mu$)} & 10000  & 100000 & \text{centi-} & 10^{-2}  \\
\text{quintamicro- (Qn$\mu$)}& 100000 & 1000000& \text{deci-}  & 10^{-1}  \\
\end{array}
$$

The micro-unit subscale preserves the same logarithmic spacing as the full prefix sequence but simplifies relative comparisons for extremely small units.

# Wimby Special Numeric Nomenclature

This section covers several standard math equations and constants for ease of reference.
## Case Signals

Wimby distinguishes the properties/parameters of Duromons and Mesomons from those of Fusomons and Peromons using the case of the symbol:
- Duromons and Mesomons use lower-case:
	- $m, r, ρ, g, vₑ$
- Fusomons and Peromons use upper-case:
	- $M, \Theta, K, R, L, V$

This is so that in equations such as:

$$
P = \sqrt{\frac{\alpha^3}{M + m}}
$$

 — it is clear from typography and context that $M$ is the mass of the star and $m$ is the mass of the planet.

If two or more bodies are referenced, numbered subscripts are employed:
- $M_1, M_2, M_3$
- $m_1, m_2, m_3$
- $R_1, R_2, R_3$
- $r_1, r_2, r_3$
- etc.

## Referents

Because the Sun, Earth, Moon, and Jupiter act as basal standards for describing the properties of other things of the same class the symbols
- $s$ ⟶ *solar* ⟶ Sun
- $t$ ⟶ _terran_ ⟶ Earth
- $l$ ⟶ _lunar_ ⟶ Moon
- $j$ ⟶ _jovian_ ⟶ Jupiter

— are used as _referents_; this avoids the need to constantly write out "solar masses", or "Earth radii"; e.g.
 - The mass of the Moon is $0.0123\,t$ or $1\,l$.
 - The mass of the Sun is $333000\,t$ and $1047\,j$.

These also simplify and standardize mathematical expressions of property assignment and relationship.

### Note — Referents in Prose vs Notation

When speaking generally or narratively, Wimby **prefers words** over bare referent symbols.  Use:
- _solar_    
- _terran_    
- _lunar_    
- _jovian_    

(all lower-case)

instead of the raw glyphs when you are **not** giving a strict numerical expression.

For example:
- **Preferred (prose):**  
    “Mars’ mass is a significant fraction of a _terran_ mass.”    
- **Avoid (prose):**  
    “Mars’ mass is a significant fraction of a $t$ mass.”    

## Combined Usages

When necessary, a property symbol may be combined with a referent to ensure clarity of meaning:
- "The resultant value is $33\,m_t$" is acceptable, since "$33_t$" does not inherently specify "mass", and could mean "radius", "gravity", or another property without specification.
### 1. Property Assignment

$$
\begin{array}{c c c}
\text{m} & = & 0.0123\,t \\
\underbrace{\text{property/parameter}}_\text{receptant} & \text{relation} & \underbrace{\text{scalar + referent}}_\text{assignant} \\
\end{array}
$$

- Receptant ⟶ that which is receiving the action of the relation
	- In this case, the symbol of the property/parameter
- Assignant ⟶ that which is assigning the action of the relation
	- In this case, the absolute value being related and the referent element donating the value

### 2. Property Relationship

$$
\begin{array}{c c c}
1\,j & = & 317.8\,t \\
\underbrace{\text{scalar + referent}}_\text{receptant} & \text{relation} & \underbrace{\text{scalar + referent}}_\text{assignant} & 
\end{array}
$$

- Receptant ⟶ that which is receiving the action of the relation
	- In this case, the relative value being related and the symbol of the element receiving the value
- Assignant ⟶ that which is assigning the action of the relation
	- In this case, the absolute value being related and the referent element donating the value


# Neolexical Symbols

- $\chi$ ⟶ chronum ⟶ $1$ Earth sidereal year ($Y_S$)

## Denominatorial Radicals
Wimby is fully aware that formal mathematics prefers to **rationalize denominators**, e.g.:

$$
\begin{aligned}
m &=\dfrac{1}{\sqrt{\rho}} \longrightarrow \dfrac{\sqrt{\rho}}{\rho} \\[0.5ex]
r &=\dfrac{v_e}{\sqrt{\rho}} \longrightarrow \dfrac{v_e\,\sqrt{\rho}}{\rho}
\end{aligned}
$$

However, WMB’s priority is **conceptual transparency**, not textbook orthodoxy.  
In many contexts — especially when illustrating how radius, density, gravity, or escape velocity scale together — it is far clearer to **leave radicals in the denominator**.

Doing so:
- preserves the visible structure of the relationship,    
- reduces algebraic clutter, and    
- keeps the expression aligned with its physical intuition.    

Therefore, WMB does **not** require rationalizing denominators unless it genuinely improves clarity — the resultant value is the same, regardless.

## Fractional Exponents and Indices
Fractional exponents are mathematically valid and compact — for example: $g={(m\,\rho^2})^{1/3}$.

However, Wimby prioritizes **structural readability** over brevity.  To make the hierarchy of operations immediately clear, WMB favors **explicit radicals with integer indices**, even in inline expressions: $g= \sqrt[3]{m\,\rho^2}$.

Using indexed radicals:
- makes the order of operations visually obvious,    
- separates the “outer” operation from the inner algebra,    
- reduces the chance of misreading nested powers, and    
- helps non-specialists parse equations without recalling exponent rules.    

Fractional exponents may still appear where they genuinely improve clarity, but **indexed radicals are the default** for WMB-style presentation.




[[Engineering Number Notation]]

# WMB Terminology: “Bio-” vs. “Animo-”

In WMB, the prefix **bio-** applies _only_ to **COH-amegic animosys** — carbon–oxygen–hydrogen biochemistry operating within the carboxyhydroxyl regime. Terms such as _biochemistry_, _biota_, _biosphere_, and _biogenic processes_ refer specifically to COH-animosys and COH-ecosystems.

An **animegic** system is one that can support or give rise to animosys of _any_ animochemical lineage, not only COH-animosys.

Common animo-terms include:
- **Animegic** — capable of supporting or generating animotes, regardless of chemical family    
- **Animotics** — study of animegic potential and animosys-systems broadly    
- **Animogenesis** — processes capable of producing animotes of any lineage    
- **Animozones** — spatial or thermodynamic regions where animosys of any chemistry may emerge or persist    
- **Animogy** — energy and metabolic principles general to all forms of animosys    
- **Animotes** — individual animosys-forms of any chemical lineage    
- **Animota** — ecological assemblies or communities of animotes (the analogue of “biota”)    
- **Animosustents** — substrates, gradients, and resources that sustain animotes of any chemistry
    
When a more specific non-COH animosys family is defined, WMB names it with its own compositional prefix (e.g., **ferro-** for iron-redox animosys, **silico-** for silicon-chain biochemistry, **azo-** for nitrogen-redox organisms, etc.).

Thus:

> **COH-animosys is the only form of animosys entitled to the “bio-” prefix. All others fall under the broader class of animo-animosys unless a more specific lineage prefix is formally defined.**

#### **Step 1: Input**
- **Obliquity ($\epsilon$)**: The planet’s axial tilt.
- **Latitude ($\phi$)**: Observer’s latitude.
- **Fraction of the zenithal year ($Z_f$)**: Time since the summer solstice, as a fraction of the year.

---

#### **Step 2: Calculate Star’s Declination**
The star’s declination varies sinusoidally over the zenithal year:

$$
\delta = \epsilon \cos(2 \pi Z_f)
$$

---

#### **Step 3: Calculate Maximum and Minimum Altitudes**
1. **Maximum Altitude ($h_\text{max}$):**
   
 $$
   h_\text{max} = 90^\circ - |\phi - \delta|
$$


1. **Minimum Altitude ($h_\text{min}$):**

$$
   h_\text{min} = 90^\circ - |\phi + \delta|
$$

---

#### **Step 4: Conditional Check**
- **If $h_\text{max} > 0^\circ$ and $(h_\text{min} < 0^\circ$:**
    - The star's path intersects the horizon. **Proceed to Step 5**.
- **Otherwise Stop:**
    - If $h_\text{min} > 0^\circ$: The star never dips below the horizon (continuous daylight).
    - If $h_\text{max} \leq 0^\circ$: The star never rises above the horizon (continuous night).

---

#### **Step 5: Calculate Center Offset ($c$)**
If the conditional in Step 4 is satisfied:

$$
c = 90^\circ - \frac{|\phi - \delta| + |\phi + \delta|}{2}
$$


---

#### **Step 6: Calculate Azimuthal Intersections**
For the star’s path intersections with the horizon ($y = 0^\circ$):

$$
x = \pm\; a\; \sqrt{1 - \frac{c^2}{b^2}}
$$

...where:
- $a = 180^\circ$ (semi-major axis),
- $b = \frac{\epsilon}{2}$ (semi-minor axis).

---

### **Key Considerations**
- **No Intersections (Skip Step 5 and 6)**:
    - If $h_\text{min} > 0^\circ$: The star’s path lies entirely above the horizon.
    - If $h_\text{max} \leq 0^\circ$: The star’s path lies entirely below the horizon.
